Trees, concrete structures used to represent search solutions.

A tree is a popular data structure that simulates a hierarchy of values or objects.

A hierarchy is an arrangement of things in which a single object is related to several other objects below it.

A tree is a connected acyclic graph, meaning that all nodes are connected but no loops, cycles exist.

In a tree, the value or object represented at a specific point is called a node.

Trees typically have a single root node with zero or more child nodes that could contain subtrees.

Let's take a deep breath and jump into some terminology.

When a node has connected nodes, the root node is called the parent.

You can apply this thinking recursively.

A child node may have its own children, which may also contain subtrees or their children.

Each child node has a single parent node.

A node without any children is a leaf node.

Trees also have a total height.

The level of specific nodes is called a depth.

The terms used to relate family members are heavily used in work with trees.

Keep this analogy in mind, it will help you connect the concepts in the tree data structure.

In Figure 2.12, the depth is measured starting from zero at the root node.

The root node has four child nodes, the tree height is four, and the depth in this example is two.

Depth is always based on where in the tree the algorithm is during traversal.

Figure 2.12.

The main attributes of a tree.

The topmost node in a tree is called the root node.

A node connected directly to one or more other nodes is called a parent node.

The nodes connected to a parent node are called child nodes or neighbors.

Nodes connected to the same parent node are called siblings.

A connection between two nodes is called an edge.

A path is a sequence of nodes and edges connecting nodes.

That aren't directly connected.

A node connected to another node by following a path away from the root node is called a descendant, and a node connected to another node by following a path toward the root node is called an ancestor.

A node with no children is called a leaf node.

The term degree describes the number of children a node has.

Therefore, a leaf node has degree zero.

Figure 2.13 represents a path from the start point to the goal for the maze problem.

This path contains nine nodes that represent different moves in the maze.

Figure 2.13.

A solution to the maze problem represented as a tree.

Trees are the fundamental data structure for many search algorithms, which we dive into in the next section.

Tip Sorting algorithms are also useful for solving certain problems and computing solutions more efficiently.

If you're interested in learning more about sorting algorithms, take a look at Groking Algorithms, second edition, by Aditya Y.

Bargava.

Uninformed search, looking blindly for solutions.

Uninformed search is also known as unguided search, blind search, or brute force search.

Uninformed search algorithms have no additional information about the domain of the problem, apart from the representation of the problem, which is usually a tree.

Think about exploring things you want to learn.

Some people might look at a wide range of topics and learn the basics of each topic, whereas other people might choose one narrow topic and explore its subtopics in depth.

These examples represent breadth first search and depth first search respectively.

Depth first search, DFS, explores a specific path from the start until it finds a goal at the utmost depth.

Breadth first search,explores all options at a specific depth before moving to options deeper in the tree.

Consider the maze scenario, figure 2.14.

In our attempt to find an optimal path to the goal, let's assume the following simple constraint to prevent an endless loop and cycles in our tree.

The player can't move into a block that they previously occupied.

Because uninformed algorithms attempt every possible option at every node, creating a cycle will cause the algorithm to fail catastrophically.

Figure 2.14.

The constraint for the maze problem,This constraint prevents cycles in the path to the goal in our scenario, but this constraint will introduce problems if, in a different maze with different constraints or rules, moving into a previously occupied block more than once is required for the optimal solution.

In Figure 2.15, all possible paths in the tree are represented to highlight the available options.

This tree contains seven paths that lead to the goal and one path that results in an invalid solution, given the constraint of not moving to previously occupied blocks.

It's important to understand that in this small maze, representing all the possibilities is feasible.

The entire point of search algorithm is not a very important thing.

Algorithms, however, is to search or generate these trees iteratively, because generating the entire tree of possibilities up front is computationally expensive and inefficient.

Figure 2.15.

All possible movement options represented as a tree.

It's also important to note that the term visiting has various meanings here.

The player visits blocks in the maze, the algorithm also visits nodes in the tree.

The order of choices influences the order of nodes being visited in the tree.

In the maze example, the priority order of movement is north, south, east, and west.

BFS.

Looking wide before looking deep.

Now that we understand the ideas behind trees and the maze example, let's explore how search algorithms can generate trees that seek out paths to the goal.

Breadth first search, BFS, is an algorithm used to traverse or generate a tree.

This algorithm starts at a specific node, usually the root, and explores every node at that depth before exploring the next depth of nodes.

Think of BFS as being like dropping a stone into a calm pond.

The ripples expand uniformly in all directions, touching everything one meter away, then everything two meters away, and so on.

The algorithm mimics this expanding ring, visiting all neighbors at the current depth before moving outward.

This guarantees that the shortest path in unweighted graphs, graphs in which all edges have the same cost, is found.

The BFS algorithm is best implemented by using a first-in, first-out, FIFO queue, in which the current depths of nodes are processed and their children are queued to be processed later.

This order of processing is exactly what we require when implementing this algorithm.

Figure 2.16 is a flowchart describing the sequence of steps involved in the algorithm.

Figure 2.16.

Flow of the BFS algorithm.

Steps of the BFS algorithm.

Let's walk through each step in the algorithm to learn exactly what operations happen.

1.

NQ root node.

The BFS algorithm is best implemented with a queue.

Objects are processed in the sequence in which they are added to the queue.

This process is known as FIFO.

The first step is adding the root node to the queue.

This node represents the starting position of the player on the map.

2.

Mark the root node as visited.

Now that the root node has been added to the queue for processing, it is marked as visited to prevent it from being revisited for no reason.

3.

Is the queue empty?

If the queue is empty, all nodes have been processed after many iterations, and if no path has been returned in step 12 of the algorithm, there is no path to the goal.

If there are still nodes in the queue, the algorithm can continue its search for the goal.

4.

Return no path to goal.

This message is the one possible exit from the algorithm if no path to the goal exists.

5.

Dequeue the node as the current node.

By pulling the next object from the queue and setting it as the current node of interest, we can explore its possibilities.

When the algorithm starts, the current node will be the root node.

6.

Get the next neighbor of the current node.

This step involves getting the next possible move in the maze from the current position by referencing the maze and determining whether north, south, east, or west movement is possible.

7.

Is the neighbor visited?

If the current neighbor hasn't been visited, it hasn't been explored yet and can be processed now.

8.

Mark the neighbor as visited.

This step indicates that this neighbor node has been visited.

9.

Set the current node as the parent of the neighbor.

This step is important for tracing the path from the current neighbor to the root node.

From a map perspective, the origin is the position the player moved from, and the current neighbor is the position the player moved to.

10.

And queue the neighbor.

The neighbor node is queued for its children to be explored later.

This queuing mechanism allows nodes from each depth to be processed in that order.

11.

Is the goal reached?

This step determines whether the current neighbor contains the goal that the algorithm is searching for.

12.

Return the path using the neighbor.

By referencing the parent of the neighbor node, then the parent of that node, and so on, the path from the goal to the root is described.

The root node will be a node without a parent.

13.

Does the current node have a next neighbor?

If the current node has more possible moves to make in the maze, jump to step 6 for that move.

Let's walk through what that process would look like in a simple tree.

As the tree is explored and nodes are added to the FIFO queue, the nodes are processed in the desired order by using the queue, figures 2.17 and 2.18.

Figure 2.17.

The sequence of tree processing using BFS,Figure 2.18.

The sequence of tree processing using BFS Exercise.

Determine the path to the solution.

What would be the order of visits using BFS for the following tree?

See this figure.

Solution.

See this figure.

In the maze example, the algorithm needs to understand the current position of the player in the maze, evaluate all possible choices for movement, and repeat that logic for each choice of movement made until the goal is reached.

By doing so, the algorithm generates a tree with a single path to the goal.

It's important to understand that the process of visiting nodes in a tree generates nodes in a tree.

We're simply finding related nodes through a mechanism.

Each path to the goal consists of a series of moves to reach the goal.

The number of moves in the path is the distance to reach the goal for that path, which we'll call the cost.

The number of moves also equals the number of nodes visited in the path, from the root node to the leaf node that contains the goal.

The algorithm moves down the tree depth by depth until it finds a goal,Then it returns the first path that got it to the goal as the solution.

A more optimal path to the goal may exist, but because BFS is uninformed, it isn't guaranteed to find that path.

In the maze example, all search algorithms used terminate when they find a solution to the goal.

It's possible to allow these algorithms to find multiple solutions with a small tweak to each algorithm, but the best use cases for search algorithms find a single goal because it's often too expensive to explore the entire tree of possibilities.

Figure 2.19 shows the generation of a tree using movements in the maze.

The BFS algorithm has explored up to depth 5 of the tree.

On the actual map, two paths to the goal emerge from the search thus far.

Figure 2.19.

Maze movement tree generation using BFS,Because the tree is generated with BFS, the algorithm generates each depth to completion before looking at the next depth.

Figure 2.20 illustrates the entire tree of possibilities, shown only for learning purposes.

At depth 7, BFS found a path to the goal, so our solution is south, south, west, west, north, north, north.

Figure 2.20.

Nodes visited in the entire tree after BFS Python code sample of the BFS algorithm.

As mentioned earlier, the BFS algorithm uses a cue to generate a tree one depth at a time.

Having a structure to store visited nodes is critical to prevent getting stuck in cyclic loops, and setting the parent of each node is important for determining a path from the starting point in the maze to the goal.

See this code.

DFS, looking deep before looking wide.

Depth First Search, DFS, is another algorithm used to traverse a tree or generate nodes and paths in a tree.

Think of DFS as being like a maze explorer who commits to a single path, walking as far as possible until hitting a dead end.

Only then do they backtrack to the last intersection to try a different turn.

Unlike BFS, which has a cautious, expanding nature, DFS is aggressive and dives deep immediately.

Figure 2.21 illustrates the general flow of the DFS algorithm.

Figure 2.21.

Flow of the DFS algorithm.

Steps of the DFS algorithm.

Let's walk through the flow of the DFS algorithm.

1.

Add the root node to the stack.

The algorithm can be implemented by using a stack in which the last object added is processed first.

This process is known as last in, first out, LIFO.

The first step is adding the root node to the stack.

2.

Is the stack empty?

If the stack is empty and no path has been returned in step eight of the algorithm, there is no path to the goal.

If there are still nodes in the stack, the algorithm can continue its search for the goal.

3.

Return no path to goal.

This return is the one possible exit from the algorithm if no path to the goal exists.

4.

Pop the node from the stack as the current node.

By pulling the next object from the stack and setting it as the current node of interest, we can explore its possibilities.

5.

Is the current node visited?

If the current node hasn't been visited, it hasn't been explored yet and can be processed now.

6.

Mark the current node as visited.

This step indicates that this node has been visited to prevent unnecessary repeat processing of it.

7.

Is the goal reached?

This step determines whether the current neighbor contains the goal the algorithm is searching for.

8.

Return the path using the current node.

By referencing the parent of the current node, then the parent of that node, and so on, the path from the goal to the root is described.

The root node will be a node without a parent.

9.

Does the current node have the next neighbor?

If the current node has more possible moves to make in the maze, that move can be added to the stack to be processed.

Otherwise, the algorithm can jump to step 2, where the next object in the stack can be processed if the stack isn't empty.

The nature of the LIFO stack allows the algorithm to process all nodes to leaf node depth before backtracking to visit other children of the root node.

10.

Set the current node as the parent of the neighbor.

This step is important for tracing the path from the current neighbor to the root node.

From a map perspective, the origin is the position the player moved from, and the current neighbor is the position the player moved to.

11.

Add the neighbor to the stack.

The neighbor node is added to the stack for its children to be explored later.

Again, this stacking mechanism allows nodes to be processed to the utmost depth before processing neighbors at shallow depths.

Figures 2.22 and 2.23 explore how the LIFO stack is used to visit nodes in the order desired by DFS.

Nodes get pushed onto and popped off the stack as the depths of the nodes visited progress.

The term push describes adding objects to a stack, and the term pop describes removing the topmost object from the stack.

Figure 2.22.

The sequence of tree processing using DFS.

Part 1.

Figure 2.23.

The sequence of tree processing using DFS.

Part 2.

Exercise.

See this figure.

Solution.

It's important to understand that the order of children matters substantially in DFS because the algorithm explores the first child until it finds leaf nodes before backtracking.

In the maze example, the order of movement, north, south, east, and west, influences the path to the goal the algorithm finds.

A change in order will result in a different solution.

The forks represented in figures 2.24 and 2.25 don't matter.

What matters is the order of the movement choices in our maze example.

In figure 2.24, unlike BFS, the DFS algorithm exploited one path, and by chance, using that path led to finding the goal with south, east, east, north, north, north, west, west, west as the solution.

Figure 2.25 shows our full tree and highlights the path taken by the DFS algorithm.

Figure 2.24.

Maze Movement Tree Generation Using DFS.

Figure 2.25.

Nodes Visited in the Entire Tree After DFS,Python Code Sample of the DFS algorithm.

Although the DFS algorithm can be implemented with a recursive function, we're looking at an implementation that is achieved with a stack to better represent the order in which nodes are visited and processed.

It's important to keep track of the visited points so that the same nodes aren't visited unnecessarily, creating cyclic loops.

See this code?

Now that we've explored both approaches, how do we choose between them?

The decision usually comes down to two factors: the need for the shortest path versus constraints on memory.

BFS is your choice when you absolutely need the shortest path, like a GPS device finding the fastest route.

This choice has a cost, however.

BFS must store every node at the current depth in memory before moving to the next.

In a massive graph, this expanding ring can consume huge amounts of RAM.

DFS is your choice when memory is limited.

Because this approach dives deep along a single path, it needs to store only the nodes on that specific path, which makes it memory efficient.

The downside is that it's not guaranteed to find the shortest path, it simply returns the first solution it bumps into.

Use cases for uninformed search algorithms.

Although the following real-world examples are technically graphs, which may contain loops or cycles, we can still use search algorithms on them by keeping track of visited nodes to avoid going in circles.

Uninformed search algorithms are versatile and useful in several real-world use cases, such as these.

Finding paths between nodes in a network.

Historically, this use case was fundamental to the creation of ARPANET, the precursor of the Internet, in 1969, when routing algorithms were needed to pass messages between the first four connected universities.

When two computers need to communicate over a network, the connection passes through many connected computers and devices.

Search algorithms can be used to establish a path in that network between two devices.

Crawling web pages.

Web searches allow us to find information on the internet across a vast number of web pages.

To index these web pages, crawlers typically read the information on each page and follow the links on the page recursively.

Search algorithms are useful for creating crawlers, metadata structures, and relationships between content.

A famous example is Google's Initial Architecture, 1998, in which Sergei Brin and Larry Page described using URL servers to feed search algorithms that crawled millions of pages to map the web structure.

Finding social network connections.

Social media applications contain many people and their relationships.

Bob may be friends with Alice, for example, but not direct friends with John, so Bob and John are indirectly related via Alice.

A social media application can suggest that Bob and John become friends because they may know each other through their mutual friendship with Alice.

A classic application is the Oracle of Bacon, a website based on the viral six degrees of Kevin Bacon meme, which uses DFS on IMDb data to find the shortest path of co-stars linking any actor to Kevin Bacon.

Optional: More about graph categories.

Graphs are useful for many computer science and mathematical problems, and due to the nature of different types of graphs, different principles and algorithms may apply to specific categories of graphs.

A graph is categorized based on its overall structure, number of nodes, number of edges, and interconnectivity between nodes.

These categories of graphs are good to know about because they're common and sometimes referenced in search and other AI algorithms.

Undirected graph: No edges are directed.

Relationships between two nodes are mutual.

Like roads between cities, roads in the graph travel in both directions.

Directed graph: Edges indicate direction.

Relationships between two nodes are explicit.

As in a graph that represents a child of a parent, the child can't be the parent of its parent.

Disconnected graph: One or more nodes are not connected by edges, as in a graph representing physical contact between continents, some nodes aren't connected.

Like continents, some nodes are connected by land, and others are separated by oceans.

A cyclic graph.

A graph that contains no cycles.

Think of university course prerequisites.

You must take Calc 101 to take Calc 201.

You can't have a situation in which a course eventually requires itself as a prerequisite.

Complete graph.

Every node is connected to every other node by an edge.

As in lines of communication in a small team, everyone talks to everyone else to collaborate.

Complete bipartite graph.

A vertex partition is a group of vertices.

Given a vertex partition, every node from one partition is connected to every node of the other partition with edges.

At a cheese tasting event, for example, every person, group A, connects with every cheese, group B, but people don't taste people, and cheese doesn't taste cheese.

Weighted graph.

A weighted graph is one in which the edges between nodes have a weight.

As in distances between cities, some are farther than others.

The connections weigh more.

It's useful to understand the types of graphs to best describe the problem and find the most efficient algorithm for processing.

Figure 2.26.

Some categories of graphs are discussed later in the book, including chapter 6 and chapter 8.

Figure 2.26 Types of Graphs Optional.

More ways to represent graphs.

Depending on the context, other encodings of graphs may be more efficient for processing or easier to work with, depending on the programming language and tools you're using.

Incidence Matrix.

An incidence matrix uses a matrix in which the height is the number of nodes and the width is the number of edges.

Each column represents a specific edge.

Why would you choose it over an adjacency list?

Discussed in the next section.

An incidence matrix is particularly useful when the edges are more important than the nodes.

In circuit design or physical flow networks, for example, we care deeply about the connections, edges.

Undirected graphs.

The column for an edge contains a one in the rows corresponding to the two connected nodes and zero everywhere else.

Directed graphs.

We use signs to indicate direction.

A common convention is to place one for the source node, where the edge starts, and minus one for the destination node, where the edge ends.

Mathematical conventions vary, some systems swap the minus one and one, but the concept of using opposite signs to denote direction remains the same.

An incidence matrix can represent both directed and undirected graphs, figure 2.27.

Figure 2.27.

Representing a graph as an incidence matrix.

Adjacency list.

An adjacency list uses linked lists in which the size of the initial list is the number of nodes in the graph, and each value represents the connected nodes for a specific node.

An adjacency list can represent both directed and undirected graphs.

Figure 2.28.

Representing a graph as an adjacency list.

Graphs are interesting, useful data structures because they can easily be represented as mathematical equations, which are the backing for all algorithms.

You can find more information about this topic throughout the book.

Summary of search fundamentals.

3. 智能搜索

第三章。

智能搜索。在本章中，理解并设计用于引导式搜索的启发式方法。

识别引导式搜索方法可能解决的问题。

理解并设计一种引导式搜索算法。

设计一种用于双人游戏的搜索算法。

定义启发式，设计有根据的猜测。

既然我们已经了解了无信息搜索算法的工作原理（第二章），我们就可以探索如何通过获取更多关于问题的信息来改进它们。

为此，我们使用有信息搜索。

有信息搜索意味着算法拥有关于所解决特定问题的一些背景信息。

它不进行盲目的广度优先或深度优先搜索。

启发式方法提供了一种表示这种关于问题的额外知识的方式。

启发式通常被称为经验法则，它是用于评估一个状态的一条规则或一组规则。

它可以定义一个状态必须满足的标准，或者衡量一个特定状态的表现。

例如，当没有明确的方法找到最优解时，我们就使用启发式。

启发式可以被解释为一种有根据的猜测。

对于所解决的问题，它更像是一个指导原则，而非科学真理。

例如，当你在餐厅点披萨时，你对它有多好吃的启发式评价可能取决于配料和饼底。

假设你喜欢在厚底脆边披萨上多加番茄酱、多加奶酪、蘑菇和菠萝。

一个拥有越多这些属性的披萨就越吸引你，并在你的启发式评价中获得更好的分数。

一个包含较少这些属性的披萨则不那么吸引人，得分也更低。

另一个例子是GPS路线规划问题。

好的启发式可能是：好路径能最小化交通耗时和行驶距离，或者好路径能最小化过路费并最大化良好路况。

一个糟糕的启发式会是最小化两点间的直线距离。

这个启发式对鸟类或飞机飞行也许可行，但对于日常通勤，我们使用道路，而道路很少在两个目的地之间提供一条直线。

让我们看一个例子，检查一个上传的音频片段是否在一个受版权保护的内容库中。

因为音频片段是声音的频率，执行这项任务的一种方法是，将上传片段的每个时间片与库中的每个片段进行比较。

这项任务需要超大量的计算。

一个初步的优化搜索方法可能会定义一个启发式，最小化两个片段之间频率分布的差异，如图3.1所示。

频率分布相同，但略有偏移。

鉴于这些分布，我们可以相当肯定该音频片段是受版权保护的。

这个解决方案可能不完美，但对于一个成本较低的算法来说，是个好的开始。

图3.1。

使用频率分布比较两个音频片段。启发式是针对特定问题的。

一个好的启发式能极大地优化解决方案。

接下来，我们将调整第二章中的迷宫场景，通过引入一个有趣的动态因素来创建启发式。

我们不再平等对待所有移动，也不再将更优解纯粹以动作更少的路径（树的浅层深度）来衡量，现在我们为不同方向的移动分配不同的成本。

对于这个例子，假设我们迷宫的重力发生了一些奇怪的改变，现在向北或向南移动的成本是向东或向西移动的五倍。

在这个调整后的迷宫场景中，影响通往目标的最佳可能路径的因素包括所采取的动作数量，以及相应路径上每个动作的成本总和。

图3.2。

迷宫示例的调整：重力。在图3.3中，树中所有可能的路径都被表示出来以突出可用选项，并标明了相应动作的成本。

再次说明，这个例子演示的是这个简单迷宫场景中的搜索空间。

它通常不适用于现实场景。

算法在搜索过程中生成树。

图3.3。

所有可能的移动选项表示为树。

迷宫问题的启发式可以定义如下：好路径能最小化移动成本，并最小化到达目标的总移动次数。

这个简单的启发式指导算法访问哪些节点，因为我们正在应用一些领域知识来解决问题。

思考实验：你能想象出什么启发式？

几个矿工专门从事不同类型的采矿，包括钻石、黄金和铂金。

所有矿工在任何矿场都有产出，但他们在与自己专长对口的矿场中开采得更快。

几个包含钻石、黄金和铂金的矿场分布在一个区域内，仓库则出现在矿场之间不同距离的地方。

如果问题是分配矿工以最大化效率并减少行程时间，你会创建什么启发式？

一个合理的启发式是将每个矿工分配给他们专长的矿场，并让他们前往离该矿场最近的仓库。

这个方案也可以解释为：最小化将矿工分配到非他们专长矿场的情况，并最小化他们前往仓库的距离。

有信息搜索：在引导下寻找方案。

有信息搜索，也称为启发式搜索，是一种将广度优先和深度优先搜索方法与一些智能结合起来的算法。

在对手头问题有一定了解的情况下，搜索由启发式引导。

根据问题的性质，我们可以采用几种有信息搜索算法，包括贪婪搜索，也称为最佳优先搜索。

然而，最流行和最有用的有信息搜索算法是A-Star。

A-star搜索。

A asterisk搜索读作A-star搜索。

A-star算法通常能提高性能，通过估计启发式方法来最小化下一个访问节点的成本。

总成本通过两个指标计算：从起始节点到当前节点的总距离，以及使用启发式方法估计的移动到特定节点的成本。

当我们试图最小化成本时，值越低，表示解决方案的表现越好。

图3.4。

图3.4。

A-Star搜索算法的函数。

A-Star之所以著名，是因为它保证能找到最短路径。

但这个保证只在你的启发式遵循一条严格规则时才成立。

永远不高估到达目标的成本。

用技术术语来说，启发式必须是可采纳的且一致的。

可采纳的。

估计的成本必须小于或等于实际成本。

可以把它看作是乐观的。

如果启发式认为目标在十英里外，而实际上只有五英里远，A星算法可能会忽略最佳路径。

一致的。

如果你搜索的是一个允许环路的图，启发式还需要是一致的，意味着估计成本不应随着你接近目标而出现不稳定的下降。

下面的处理示例是一个抽象例子，演示了在启发式引导下搜索并访问一棵树的过程。

重点是树中各个节点的启发式计算。

广度优先搜索，BFS，在进入下一个深度之前，访问当前深度的所有节点。

深度优先搜索一直向下访问节点直到最终深度，然后回溯到根节点并访问下一条路径。

A星搜索的不同之处在于它没有预设的遵循模式。

节点根据其启发式成本按顺序访问。

为实现这一点，A星算法使用一个优先队列来管理它需要探索的节点。

与栈（后进先出，LIFO）或标准队列（先进先出，FIFO）不同，优先队列自动将最有希望的节点保持在前端，确保算法总是优先处理估计总成本最低的节点。

然而，算法一开始并不知道所有节点的成本。

成本是在探索或生成树的过程中计算的，每个访问过的节点都会被加入这个队列，这意味着成本高于已访问节点的节点会被忽略，从而节省计算时间。

图3.5、3.6和3.7。

图3.5。

使用A星搜索的树处理序列。

第一部分。

图3.6。

使用A星搜索的树处理序列。

第二部分。

图3.7。

A星搜索算法的流程。

A星搜索的步骤。

让我们一步步走完A星搜索算法的流程。

一。

将根节点加入优先队列。

A星算法使用一个优先队列，通常实现为最小堆，以确保我们总是先探索最有希望的路径。

第一步是将根节点加入这个队列。

二。

优先队列是否为空？

如果队列为空，且算法的第八步未返回路径，则不存在通往目标的路径。

如果队列中仍有节点，算法可以继续搜索。

三。

返回无路径到目标。

如果不存在通往目标的路径，此步骤是算法的一个可能出口。

四。

从优先队列中弹出成本最低的对象，作为当前节点。

通过从队列中取出成本最低的对象并将其设为当前感兴趣的节点，我们可以探索它的可能性。

五。

当前节点是否已访问过？

如果当前节点尚未被访问，说明它还未被探索，现在可以处理。

六。

将当前节点标记为已访问。

此步骤表明该节点已被访问，以防不必要的重复处理。

七。

是否已到达目标？

此步骤确定当前邻居是否包含算法正在搜索的目标。

八。

利用当前节点返回路径。

引用当前节点的父节点，然后是那个节点的父节点，依此类推，就描述了从目标到根的路径。

根节点是没有父节点的节点。

九。

当前节点是否有下一个邻居？

如果在迷宫示例中，当前节点还有更多可能的移动，那个移动可以被添加以作处理。

否则，算法可以跳转到第二步，如果优先队列不为空，可以处理其中的下一个对象。

优先队列的特性允许算法在分支间切换。

如果加入了一个比当前路径成本更低的邻居，算法会有效地跳转到那个更有希望的节点，接着进行探索。

十。

让优先队列为节点排序。

因为我们使用的是优先队列，总成本最低的节点会自动浮到顶部。

我们不需要手动排序。

这个结构确保我们弹出的下一个节点始终是最佳候选。

十一。

将当前节点设为其邻居的父节点。

此步骤对于追踪从当前邻居到根节点的路径很重要。

从地图视角看，起点是玩家移动前的位置，当前邻居是玩家移动到的位置。

十二。

计算邻居的成本。

成本函数对于引导A星算法至关重要。

成本是通过将到根节点的距离与下一步移动的启发式得分相加来计算的。

更智能的启发式能直接影响A星算法，使其表现更佳。

十三。

将邻居加入优先队列。

该邻居节点被加入优先队列，以便随后探索它的子节点。

与DFS不同，A星搜索根据子节点估计的总成本来确定它们的顺序。

如果两个节点成本相同，通常先加入的节点先被访问。

接下来的三张图阐明了树处理的序列。

处理序列。

在图3.8和3.9中，A星算法为了找到一个解，既广度式也深度式地访问节点。

图3.10展示了几条通往目标的路径，但A星算法在最小化成本的同时找到了一条通往目标的路径，这条路径既有更少的移动次数，也有更便宜的移动成本，因为根据我们为此场景添加的重力规则，向北和向南的移动更昂贵。

优先队列自动将最有希望的节点保持在前端，确保算法总是处理估计总成本最低的节点。

计算成本的函数至关重要。

为了让A星工作，我们计算两个值：迄今为止的实际成本，记作G，和启发式估计值，记作H。

下面的函数描述了这些成本是如何得出的。

在我们的迷宫示例中，我们使用曼哈顿距离启发式，计算总的网格步数，来估计剩余距离。

见此代码。

无信息搜索算法，如BFS，会穷尽式地探索各个层，并在无权图中得出最优解。

DFS虽然有用，但不保证能找到最短路径。

当你能创建一个合理的启发式时，A星搜索是一种更优的方法。

它比无信息算法计算效率更高，因为它忽略了估计成本高的节点。

然而，如果启发式有缺陷或不可采纳（过于悲观），A星算法就无法再保证找到最优解。

有信息搜索算法的用例。

有信息搜索算法用途广泛，适用于许多可以定义启发式的现实场景，例如以下这些：电子游戏中自主游戏角色的寻路。

游戏开发者经常使用这种算法来控制游戏中敌方单位的移动，其目标是在环境中找到人类玩家。

自然语言处理（NLP）中的文本生成与解码。

当AI模型（如聊天机器人）编写句子时，它们不是只挑选单个最可能的下一个词。

它们通常使用启发式搜索，比如束搜索，来向前查看一个句子的多个可能分支，并找到在语法和逻辑上最合理的单词序列。

电信网络中的路由。

引导式搜索算法可以为电信网络中的网络流量找到最短路径，以提高性能。

服务器或网络节点及连接可以表示为可搜索的节点和边构成的图。

解决单人游戏和谜题。

有信息搜索算法可以解决单人游戏和谜题，如魔方，因为每一步都是在可能性树中的一个决策，直到找到目标状态。

对抗性搜索：在变化的环境中寻找方案。

迷宫游戏只涉及一个行动者：玩家。

环境只受单个玩家的影响，所以该玩家产生了所有可能性。

到目前为止，目标一直是通过选择距离最短、成本最低的通往目标的路径，来最大化玩家的收益。

对抗性搜索的特点是存在对立或冲突。

对抗性问题要求我们预测、理解并反制对手的行动，以追求目标。

对抗性问题的例子包括两人回合制游戏，如井字棋、四子棋和国际象棋。

玩家轮流尝试改变游戏环境的状态，使之对自己有利。

一组规则规定了玩家如何改变环境，以及什么是胜利和结束状态。

这类游戏通常被称为零和游戏。

一个玩家要赢（正十），另一个玩家就必须输（负十）。

结果的总和永远为零。

本节使用四子棋游戏来探索对抗性问题。

四子棋，图3.11，由一个网格组成，玩家轮流将棋子丢入其中一列。

特定列中的棋子会堆叠起来，任何成功创建四个己方棋子的相邻序列（垂直、水平或对角线）的玩家获胜。

如果网格已满，没有胜者，则游戏平局。

图3.11四子棋游戏。极小化极大搜索。

模拟行动，选择最佳未来。

极小化极大搜索旨在基于每个玩家可能采取的移动，构建一棵可能结果的树，它偏向于对智能体有利的路径，并避免有利于对手的路径。

这种搜索模拟可能的移动，假设双方都完美地游戏，即对手总是选择最伤害智能体的那步棋，并在做出相应移动后，根据启发式对状态评分。

极小化极大搜索试图发现尽可能多的未来状态，但由于内存和计算的限制，发现整个游戏树可能不现实，所以它搜索到一个指定的深度。

极小化极大搜索模拟各玩家轮流走棋，因此指定的深度与双方玩家走的回合数直接相关。

例如，深度为四意味着每位玩家已经走了两回合。

玩家A走一步，玩家B走一步，玩家A再走一步，玩家B再走一步。

极小化极大算法使用启发式得分来做决策。

这个得分由精心设计的启发式定义，而非由算法自动学习。

如果我们有一个特定的游戏状态，从该状态出发的每一个可能的有效移动结果都将是游戏树中的一个子节点。

想象一个提供分数的启发式，其中正数优于负数。

通过模拟每一个可能的有效移动，极小化极大搜索算法会最小化那些给对手带来优势或获胜状态的移动，并最大化那些给智能体带来优势或获胜状态的移动。

图3.12展示了一棵极小化极大搜索树。

在此图中，叶节点是唯一计算启发式得分的节点，因为这些状态表示胜利或平局。

其他节点表示进行中的状态。

从计算启发式的深度开始向上移动，根据在未来模拟状态中接下来轮到谁走棋，会选出得分最小的子节点或得分最大的子节点。

从顶部开始，智能体试图最大化自己的得分。

在每次交替轮次后，意图会发生改变，因为目标是最大化智能体的得分，并最小化对手的得分。

然后，它确定每个状态下的所有可能移动，直到找到最有利的路径。

使得智能体获胜的游戏状态返回十分，使得对手获胜的状态返回负十分。

极小化极大搜索的步骤。

让我们一步步走完极小化极大搜索算法的流程。

一。

给定一个游戏状态、当前模式是最大化还是最小化，以及当前深度，算法开始。

理解算法的输入很重要，因为极小化极大搜索算法是递归的。

递归算法在其一步或多步中调用自身，并且必须有一个退出条件以防止它无限地调用自身。

二。

当前节点是终局状态还是深度为零？

此条件判断游戏的当前状态是终局状态，还是已达到期望的深度。

终局状态是指一个玩家获胜或游戏平局的状态。

十分代表智能体获胜，负十分代表对手获胜，零分表示平局。

指定深度是因为遍历整棵可能性树至所有终局状态计算成本高昂，在普通计算机上很可能耗时过长。

通过指定深度，算法可以向前看几个回合，判断是否存在终局状态。

三。

返回当前得分和上一手棋。

如果当前状态是终局游戏状态或已到达指定深度，则返回当前状态的得分。

四。

当前模式是否为最大化？

如果算法的当前迭代处于最大化状态，它会尝试最大化智能体的得分。

五。

将已知最佳得分设为正无穷大。

如果当前模式是最小化得分，最佳得分设为正无穷大，因为我们知道游戏状态返回的得分总是会更小。

在实际实现中，会用一个非常大的数代替无穷大。

六。

将已知最佳得分设为负无穷大。

如果当前模式是最大化得分，最佳得分设为负无穷大，因为我们知道游戏状态返回的得分总是会更大。

在实际实现中，会用一个非常大的负数代替负无穷大。

七。

获取当前游戏状态下的所有可能移动。

此步骤指定了在当前游戏状态下可以做出的可能移动的列表。

随着游戏进展，开始时可用的一些移动可能不再可用。

在四子棋例子中，一列可能已被填满。

因此，选择该列的移动是无效的。

八。

是否还有下一个有效移动？

如果没有可能的移动被模拟过，且没有更多有效移动可做，算法会短路，返回该函数调用实例中的最佳移动。

九。

将当前游戏状态复制为游戏n。

需要一份当前游戏状态的副本，以便算法能在其上执行对未来可能移动的模拟。

十。

通过将其应用于游戏状态n，来模拟当前移动。

此步骤将当前感兴趣的移动应用于复制的游戏状态。

十一。

按需设置best n。

递归在这一步发挥作用。

best n是一个用来存储下一个最佳移动的变量，我们正让算法从这个移动开始探索未来的可能性。

十二。

当前模式是否为最大化？

当递归调用返回一个最佳候选时，此条件判断当前模式是否最大化得分。

十三。

是否best n小于已知最佳？

如果当前模式是最大化得分，此步骤判断算法是否找到了比以前更好的得分。

十四。

是否best n大于已知最佳？

如果当前模式是最小化得分，此步骤判断算法是否找到了比以前更好的得分。

十五。

将已知最佳设为best n。

如果找到了新的最佳得分，就将已知最佳设为那个分数。

以四子棋在特定状态下的例子来说，极小化极大搜索算法生成了图3.16所示的树。

从起始状态开始，探索了每一种可能的移动。

然后，从该状态出发的每一步都被探索，直到找到一个终局状态。

即棋盘已满，或有玩家获胜。

因为算法旨在最大化自己的得分，所以正数是需要的，而对手的胜利则用负数计分。

通过跟踪所选的节点和分数，并直观地将自己代入问题，我们看到算法选择了一条通往平局的路径，以防止失败。

如果算法选择通往胜利的路径，那么下一回合失败的可能性就很高。

该算法假设对手总是会走最聪明的那步棋，来最大化他们获胜的机会。

极小化极大搜索的Python代码示例。

极小化极大搜索算法被实现为一个递归函数。

该函数接收当前状态、期望的搜索深度、最小化或最大化模式以及上一手棋。

算法通过为树中每一深度的每个子节点返回最佳移动和得分来终止。

将此代码与图3.15中的流程图比较，我们看到那些检查当前模式是最大化还是最小化的繁琐条件看起来不那么明显了。

在代码中，一或负一分别代表最大化或最小化的意图。

通过使用一些巧妙的逻辑，最佳得分、条件和状态切换可以通过负数乘法原理完成，即一个负数乘以另一个负数的结果为正。

因此，如果负一表示对手的回合，将其乘以负一结果为一，这表示智能体的回合。

然后，在下一回合，一乘以负一结果为负一，再次表示对手的回合。

Alpha-beta剪枝：仅通过探索合理路径来优化。

Alpha-beta剪枝是一种与极小化极大搜索算法一起使用的技术，用于短接对已知会产生较差解决方案的游戏树区域的探索。

此技术优化了极小化极大搜索算法以节省计算，因为无关紧要的路径被忽略了。

假设你正在用地图寻找最快的回家路线。

路线A耗时三十分钟。

接着，你检查路线B。

应用告诉你，路线B的第一段涉及一趟耗时四十五分钟的轮渡。

你立即停止计算路线B。

你不需要知道轮渡之后的路况有多快，因为仅第一步就已经长于路线A的全程，路线B注定更差。

你将那条路线从你的计算中剪枝掉。

因为我们知道四子棋游戏树如何爆炸式增长，我们清楚地看到，忽略更多的路径将显著提高计算性能。

图3.22。

Alpha-beta剪枝算法的工作原理是，将最大化玩家的最佳得分和最小化玩家的最佳得分分别存储为alpha和beta。

初始时，alpha设为负无穷大，beta设为无穷大，即每位玩家的最差得分。

如果最小化玩家的最佳得分小于最大化玩家的最佳得分，那么从逻辑上讲，已访问节点的其他子路径不会影响最佳得分。

图3.22。

Alpha-beta剪枝的一个例子。

图3.23说明了为进一步优化alpha-beta剪枝，而对极小化极大搜索流程所做的修改。

阴影块是极小化极大搜索算法流程中增加的步骤。

图3.23。

带有alpha-beta剪枝的极小化极大搜索算法流程。

带有alpha-beta剪枝的极小化极大搜索的步骤。

以下步骤是对极小化极大搜索算法的补充，当找到的最佳得分不改变结果时，允许终止对路径的探索。

一。

当前模式是否为最大化？

再次判断算法当前是试图最大化还是最小化得分。

二。

是否best n大于或等于alpha？

如果当前模式是最大化得分，且当前最佳得分best n大于alpha，我们就找到了一条比以前更好的路径。

我们更新alpha以反映这个新的更高值。

此步骤收紧了搜索窗口，可能帮助我们剪枝掉未来的分支，但我们此时还不会停止探索这个节点，除非在第四步alpha大于或等于beta。

三。

将alpha设为best n。

此步骤将变量alpha设为best n。

四。

是否alpha大于或等于beta？

该得分与其他已找到的得分一样好，对此节点剩下的探索可以通过中断来忽略。

五。

是否best n小于或等于beta？

如果当前模式是最小化得分，且当前最佳得分best n小于beta，我们就为最小化玩家找到了一步更强的棋。

我们更新beta以反映这个新的更低值。

如果最大化玩家的最佳选项alpha触及了这个新界限，此步骤将从上方收紧搜索窗口，帮助我们剪枝掉未来的分支。

六。

将beta设为best n。

此步骤将变量beta设为best n。

七。

是否alpha大于或等于beta？

该得分与其他已找到的得分一样好，对此节点剩下的探索可以通过中断来忽略。

当选择最小化min时，变量min或max为负一，当选择最大化max时，变量min或max为一。

英文原文

Chapter 3.

Intelligent Search In this chapter, understanding and designing heuristics for guided search.

Identifying problems that guided search approaches may solve.

Understanding and designing a guided search algorithm.

Designing a search algorithm to play a two-player game.

Defining heuristics, designing educated guesses.

Now that we have an idea of how uninformed search algorithms work, chapter 2, we can explore how to improve them by seeing more information about the problem.

For this task, we use informed search.

Informed search means that the algorithm has some context about the specific problem being solved.

It doesn't conduct a blind breadth-first or depth-first search.

Heuristics provide a way to represent this additional knowledge about the problem.

Often called a rule of thumb, a heuristic is a rule or set of rules used to evaluate a state.

It can define criteria that a state must satisfy or measure the performance of a specific state.

For example, we use a heuristic when no clear method of finding an optimal solution is available.

A heuristic can be interpreted as an educated guess.

It's more a guideline than a scientific truth with respect to the problem being solved.

When you order a pizza at a restaurant, for example, your heuristic of how good it is may be defined by the ingredients and base.

Suppose that you enjoy extra tomato sauce, extra cheese, mushrooms, and pineapple on a thick base with a crunchy crust.

A pizza that has more of these attributes will be more appealing to you and achieve a better score for your heuristic.

A pizza that contains fewer of those attributes will be less appealing and achieve a poorer score.

Another example is a GPS routing problem.

The heuristic may be good paths minimize time in traffic and distance traveled, or good paths minimize toll fees and maximize good road conditions.

A poor heuristic would minimize the straight line distance between two points.

This heuristic might work for bird or airplane flight, but for everyday commuting, we use roads, which rarely offer a straight line between two destinations.

Let's look at an example, checking whether an uploaded audio clip is in a library of copyrighted content.

Because audio clips are frequencies of sound, one way to perform this task is to compare every time slice of the uploaded clip against every clip in the library.

This task requires an extreme amount of computation.

A primitive approach to a better search might define a heuristic that minimizes the difference in distribution of frequencies between two clips, as shown in Figure 3.1.

The frequencies are identical but shifted slightly.

Given these distributions, we can be fairly certain that the audio clip is copyrighted.

This solution may not be perfect, but it's a good start on a less expensive algorithm.

Figure 3.1.

Comparison of two audio clips using frequency distribution Heuristics are problem specific.

And a good heuristic optimizes solutions substantially.

Next, we'll adjust the maze scenario from chapter 2 to create heuristics by introducing an interesting dynamic.

Instead of treating all movements the same way and measuring better solutions purely in terms of paths with fewer actions, shallow depth in the tree, now we assign different costs to movements in different directions.

For this example, suppose that some strange shift in the gravity of our maze has occurred, and moving north or south now costs five times as much as moving east or west.

In the adjusted maze scenario, the factors that influence the best possible path to the goal are the number of actions taken and the sum of the cost of each action on a respective path.

Figure 3.2.

Adjustments to the maze example: Gravity In Figure 3.3, all possible paths in the tree are represented to highlight the available options, indicating the costs of the respective actions.

Again, this example demonstrates the search space in the trivial maze scenario.

It doesn't often apply to real-life scenarios.

The algorithm generates the tree as part of the search.

Figure 3.3.

All possible movement options are represented as a tree.

A heuristic for the maze problem can be defined as follows: Good paths minimize the cost of movement and minimize total moves to the goal.

This simple heuristic guides which nodes are visited because we're applying some domain knowledge to solve the problem.

Thought experiment: What heuristic can you imagine?

Several miners specialize in different types of mining, including diamond, gold, and platinum.

All the miners are productive in any mine, but they mine faster in mines that align with their specialties.

Several mines that contain diamonds, gold, and platinum are spread across an area, and depots appear at different distances between mines.

If the problem is distributing miners to maximize efficiency and reduce travel time, what heuristic would you create?

A sensible heuristic would assign each miner to a mine of their specialty and tasking them with traveling to the depot closest to that mine.

This solution can also be interpreted as minimizing assigning miners to mines that are not their specialty and minimizing the distance they travel to depots.

Informed search, looking for solutions with guidance.

Informed search, also known as heuristic search, is an algorithm that uses the breadth-first and depth-first search approaches combined with some intelligence.

The search is guided by heuristics, given some knowledge of the problem at hand.

We can employ several informed search algorithms, depending on the nature of the problem, including greedy search, also known as best-first search.

The most popular and useful informed search algorithm, however, is A-Star.

A-star search.

A asterisk search is pronounced A-star search.

The A-star algorithm usually improves performance.

By estimating heuristics to minimize the cost of the next node visited.

The total cost is calculated with two metrics: the total distance from the start node to the current node and the estimated cost of moving to a specific node by using a heuristic.

When we attempt to minimize cost, a lower value indicates a better performing solution.

Figure 3.4.

Figure 3.4.

The function for the A-Star search algorithm.

A-Star is famous because it guarantees that it will find the shortest path.

But this guarantee holds true only if your heuristic follows a strict rule.

Never overestimate the cost to the goal.

In technical terms, the heuristic must be admissible and consistent.

Admissible.

The estimated cost must be less than or equal to the actual cost.

Think of it as being optimistic.

If the heuristic thinks that the goal is 10 miles away when it's actually 5 miles away, ASTAR might ignore the best path.

Consistent.

If you're searching a graph, which allows loops, the heuristic also needs to be consistent, meaning that the estimated cost shouldn't drop erratically as you get closer to the goal.

The following example of processing is an abstract example of visiting a tree with heuristics guiding the search.

The focus is on the heuristic calculations for the various nodes of the tree.

Breadth First Search, BFS, visits all nodes on each depth before moving to the next depth.

Depth First Search visits all nodes down to the final depth before traversing back to the root and visiting the next path.

A star search is different in that it doesn't have a predefined pattern to follow.

Nodes are visited in an order based on their heuristic costs.

To achieve this task, the A star algorithm uses a priority queue to manage the nodes it needs to explore.

Unlike a stack, last in, first out, LIFO, or a standard queue, first in, first out, FIFO, a priority queue automatically keeps the most promising node at the front, ensuring that the algorithm always processes the node with the lowest estimated total cost next.

The algorithm doesn't know the costs of all nodes up front, however.

Costs are calculated as the tree is explored or generated, and each node visited is added to this queue, which means that nodes that cost more than already visited nodes are ignored, saving computation time.

Figures 3.5, 3.6, and 3.7.

Figure 3.5.

The sequence of tree processing using ASTAR search.

Part 1.

Figure 3.6.

The sequence of tree processing using ASTAR Search.

Part 2.

Figure 3.7.

Flow of the A-Star Search Algorithm.

Steps in A-Star Search.

Let's walk through the flow of the A-Star search algorithm.

1.

Add the root node to the priority queue.

The A-Star algorithm uses a priority.

queue, often implemented as a minheap, to ensure that we always explore the most promising path first.

The first step is adding the root node to this queue.

2.

Is the priority queue empty?

If the queue is empty and no path is returned in step 8 of the algorithm, there is no path to the goal.

If there are still nodes in the queue, the algorithm can continue its search.

3.

Return no path to goal.

This step is the one possible exit from the algorithm if no path to the goal exists.

4.

Pop the lowest cost object from the priority queue as the current node.

By pulling the lowest cost object from the queue and setting it as the current node of interest, we can explore its possibilities.

5.

Has the current node been visited?

If the current node hasn't been visited, it hasn't been explored and can be processed now.

6.

Mark the current node as visited.

This step indicates that this node has been visited to prevent unnecessary repeat processing.

7.

Has the goal been reached?

This step determines whether the current neighbor contains the goal the algorithm is searching for.

8.

Return the path using the current node.

Referencing the parent of the current node, then the parent of that node, and so on describes the path from the goal to the root.

The root node is a node without a parent.

9.

Does the current node have a next neighbor?

If the current node has more possible moves to make in the maze example, that move can be added to be processed.

Otherwise, the algorithm can jump to step 2, in which the next object in the priority queue can be processed if it isn't empty.

The nature of the priority queue allows the algorithm to switch between branches.

If a neighbor that's cheaper than the current path is added, the algorithm effectively jumps to explore that more promising node next.

10.

Let the priority queue order the nodes.

Because we're using a priority queue, the node with the lowest total cost effectively floats to the top.

We don't need to sort manually.

The structure ensures that the next node we pop is always the best candidate.

11.

Set the current node as the parent of the neighbor.

This step is important for tracing the path from the current neighbor to the root node.

From a map perspective, the origin is the position the player moved from, and the current neighbor is the position the player moved to.

12.

Calculate the cost for the neighbor.

The cost function is critical for guiding the A-star algorithm.

The cost is calculated by summing the distance from the root node with the heuristic score for the next move.

More intelligent heuristics directly influence the A-star algorithm for better performance.

13.

Add the neighbor to the priority queue.

The neighbor node is added to the priority queue for its children to be explored later.

Unlike DFS, A-star search determines the order of child nodes by their estimated total costs.

If two nodes have the same cost, the first node added is usually visited first.

The next three figures illustrate the tree process.

Processing sequence.

The A-Star algorithm uses a priority queue to manage the nodes it needs to explore.

A priority queue automatically keeps the most promising node at the front, ensuring that the algorithm always processes the node with the lowest estimated total cost.

The functions for calculating the cost are critical.

For A-star to work, we calculate two values, the actual cost so far, G, and the heuristic estimate, H.

The following functions describe how these costs are derived.

In our maze example, we use a Manhattan distance heuristic, measuring total grid steps, to estimate the remaining distance.

See this code.

Uninformed search algorithms such as BFS explore layers exhaustively and result in the optimal solution, in unweighted graphs.

DFS, although useful, doesn't guarantee the shortest path.

A-star search is a superior approach when you can create a sensible heuristic.

It computes more efficiently than uninformed algorithms because it ignores nodes with high estimated costs.

If the heuristic is flawed or inadmissible, overly pessimistic, however, A-star can no longer guarantee finding the optimal solution.

Use cases for informed search algorithms.

Informed search algorithms are versatile and useful for many real-world use cases in which heuristics can be defined, such as the following: Pathfinding for autonomous game characters in video games.

Game developers often use this algorithm to control the movement of enemy units in a game in which the goal is to find the human player within an environment.

Generating text and decoding in natural language processing, NLP.

When AI models, such as chatbots, write sentences, they don't pick the single most likely next word.

They often use heuristic search, such as beam search, to look ahead at multiple possible branches of a sentence and find the sequence of words that makes the most sense grammatically and logically.

Routing in telecommunications networks.

Guided search algorithms can find the shortest paths for network traffic in telecommunications.

Networks to improve performance.

Servers or network nodes and connections can be represented as searchable graphs of nodes and edges.

Solving single-player games and puzzles.

Informed search algorithms can solve single-player games and puzzles such as the Rubik's Cube, because each move is a decision in a tree of possibilities until the goal state is found.

Adversarial Search, looking for solutions in a changing environment.

The maze game involves a single actor, the player.

The environment is affected only by the single player, so that player generates all possibilities.

Up to now, the goal has been to maximize the benefit for the player by choosing the paths to the goal with the shortest distance and cost.

Adversarial search is characterized by opposition or conflict.

Adversarial problems require us to anticipate, understand, and counteract the actions of an opponent in pursuit of a goal.

Examples of adversarial problems include two-player turn-based games such as tic-tac-toe, connect 4, and chess.

The players take turns trying to affect the state of the game's environment in their favor.

A set of rules dictates how players can change the environment and what the winning and end states are.

Games of this type are often called zero-sum games.

For one player to win, plus ten, the other must lose, minus ten.

The total sum of the outcome is always zero.

This section uses the Connect4 game to explore adversarial problems.

Connect 4, figure 3.11, consists of a grid in which players take turns dropping tokens into one of the columns.

The tokens in a specific column pile up, and any player who manages to create four adjacent sequences of their tokens, vertically, horizontally, or diagonally, wins.

If the grid is full, with no winner, the game results in a draw.

Figure 3.11 The Connect 4 Game Minimax Search.

Simulate actions and choose the best future.

Minimax search aims to build a tree of possible outcomes based on moves that each player could make, favoring paths that are advantageous to the agent and avoiding paths that are favorable to the opponent.

This type of search simulates possible moves assuming that both players play perfectly, that is, the opponent always chooses the move that hurts the agent most, and scores the state based on a heuristic after making the respective move.

Minimax search attempts to discover as many future states as possible, but due to memory and computation limitations, discovering the entire game tree may not be realistic, so it searches to a specified depth.

Minimax search simulates the turns taken by each player, so the depth specified is linked directly to the number of turns taken by both players.

A depth of four, for example, means that each player has had two turns.

Player A makes a move, player B makes a move, player A makes another move, and player B makes another move.

The Minimax algorithm uses a heuristic score to make decisions.

This score is defined by a crafted heuristic, not learned automatically by the algorithm.

If we have a specific game state, every possible valid outcome of a move from that state will be a child node in the game tree.

Imagine a heuristic that provides a score in which positive numbers are better than negative numbers.

By simulating every possible valid move, the Minimax search algorithm minimizes moves that give the opponent an advantage or a winning state and maximizes moves that give the agent an advantage or a winning state.

Figure 3.12 illustrates a minimax search tree.

In this figure, the leaf nodes are the only nodes for which the heuristic score is calculated, because these states indicate a win or a draw.

The other nodes indicate states that are in progress.

Starting at the depth where the heuristic is calculated and moving upward, either the child with the minimum score or the child with the maximum score is chosen, depending on whose turn is next in the future simulated states.

Starting at the top, the agent attempts to maximize its score.

After each alternating turn, the intention changes because the aim is to maximize the agent's score and minimize the opponent's score.

Figure 3.12.

The sequence of tree processing using Minimax Search.

Exercise.

Solution.

See this figure.

Because the Minimax search algorithm simulates possible outcomes in games that offer a multitude of choices, the game tree explodes, and exploring the entire tree quickly becomes too computationally expensive.

In the simple example of Connect 4 played on a 5x4 block board, the number of possibilities already makes exploring the entire game tree on every turn inefficient.

Figure 3.13.

Figure 3.13.

The explosion of possibilities while searching the game tree.

To use Minimax search in the Connect4 example, the algorithm essentially makes all possible moves from a current game state.

Then it determines all possible moves from each state until it finds the most favorable path.

Game states that result in a win for the agent return a score of 10, and states that result in a win for the opponent return a score of minus 10.

Minimax search tries to maximize the agent's positive score.

Figures 3.14 and 3.15.

Although the flowchart for the min-max search algorithm looks complex due to its size, it really isn't.

The number of conditions that check whether the current state is to maximize or minimize causes the chart to bloat.

Figure 3.14.

Scoring for the agent versus scoring for the opponent.

Figure 3.15.

Flow of the Minimax Search Algorithm.

Steps in Minimax Search.

Let's walk through the flow of the Minimax search algorithm.

1.

Given a game state, whether the current mode is minimization or maximization, and a current depth, the algorithm starts.

It's important to understand the inputs for the algorithm, because the Minimax search algorithm is recursive.

A recursive algorithm calls itself in one or more of its steps, and must have an exit condition to prevent it from calling itself forever.

2.

Is the current node an end state or is depth 0?

This condition determines whether the current state of the game is a terminal state or whether the desired depth has been reached.

A terminal state is one in which one player has won or the game is a draw.

A score of 10 represents a win for the agent, a score of minus 10 represents a win for the opponent, and a score of 0 indicates a draw.

A depth is specified because traversing the entire tree of possibilities to all end states is computationally expensive and is likely to take too long on the average computer.

By specifying a depth, the algorithm can look a few turns into the future to determine whether a terminal state exists.

3.

Return the current score and the last move.

The score of the current state is returned if the current state is a terminal game state or the specified depth has been reached.

4.

Is the current mode max?

If the current iteration of the algorithm is in the maximize state, it tries to maximize the agent's score.

5.

Set the best known score as plus infinity.

If the current mode minimizes the score, the best score is set to positive infinity, because we know that the scores returned by the game states will always be less.

In actual implementation, a very large number is used instead of infinity.

6.

Set the best known score as minus infinity.

If the current mode maximizes the score, the best score is set to negative infinity, because we know that the scores returned by the game states will always be more.

In actual implementation, a very large negative number is used instead of infinity.

7.

Get all possible moves given the current game state.

This step specifies a list of possible moves that can be made given the current game state.

As the game progresses, some moves that were available at the start may not be available anymore.

In the Connect 4 example, a column may be filled.

Therefore, a move that selects that column is invalid.

8.

Has the next valid move been made?

If no possible moves have been simulated and there are no more valid moves to make, the algorithm short circuits to returning the best move in that instance of the function call.

9.

Copy the current game state as game n.

A copy of the current game state is required so the algorithm can perform simulations of possible future moves on it.

10.

Simulate the current move by applying it to game state game n.

This step applies the current move of interest to the copied game state.

11.

Set best n as the required.

Recursion comes into play in this step.

Best n is a variable used to store the next best move, and we're making the algorithm explore future possibilities from this move.

12.

Is the current mode max?

When the recursive call returns a best candidate, this condition determines whether the current mode maximizes the score.

13.

Is best n less than the known best?

This step determines whether the algorithm has found a better score than one previously found if the mode maximizes the score.

14.

Is best n greater than the known best?

This step determines whether the algorithm has found a better score than one previously found, if the mode minimizes the score.

15.

Set the known best as best n.

If the new best score is found, set the known best as that score.

Given the Connect4 example at a specific state, the Minimax search algorithm generates the tree shown in Figure 3.16.

From the start state, every possible move is explored.

Then each move from that state is explored until a terminal state is found.

The board is full, or a player has won.

Figure 3.16.

A representation of the possible states in a Connect4 game.

The highlighted nodes in Figure 3.17 are terminal state nodes in which draws are scored as zero, losses are scored as minus 10, and wins are scored as 10.

Because the algorithm aims to maximize its score, a positive number is required, whereas opponent wins are scored with a negative number.

Figure 3.17.

The possible end states in a Connect4 game.

When these scores are known, the Minimax algorithm starts at the lowest depth and chooses the node whose score is the minimum value.

Figure 3.18.

Then, at the next depth, the algorithm chooses the node whose score is the maximum value.

Figure 3.19.

Figure 3.18.

The possible scores for end states in a Connect4 game.

Part 1.

Figure 3.19.

The possible scores for end states in a Connect4 game.

Part 2.

Finally, at the next depth, nodes whose score is the minimum are chosen, and the root node chooses the maximum of the options.

By following the nodes and scores selected and intuitively applying ourselves to the problem, we see that the algorithm selects a path to a draw to prevent a loss.

If the algorithm selects the path to a win, there is a high likelihood of a loss in the next turn.

The algorithm assumes that the opponent will always make the smartest move to maximize their chance of winning.

Figure 3.20.

The simplified tree in Figure 3.21 represents the outcome of the Minimax search algorithm for the given game state example.

Figure 3.20.

The possible scores for end states in a Connect 4 game.

Part 3.

Figure 3.21.

Simplified Game Tree with Minimax Scoring.

Python Code Sample for Minimax Search.

The Minimax search algorithm is implemented as a recursive function.

The function is given the current state, desired depth to search, minimization or maximization mode, and last move.

The algorithm terminates by returning the best move and score for every child at every depth in the tree.

Comparing the code with the flow chart in Figure 3.15, we see that the tedious conditions of checking whether the current mode is maximizing or minimizing are not as apparent.

In the code, 1 or minus 1 represents the intention to maximize or minimize, respectively.

By using some clever logic, the best score, conditions, and switching states can be done via the principle of negative multiplication, in which a negative number multiplied by another negative number results in a positive.

So if minus 1 indicates the opponent's turn, multiplying it by minus 1 results in 1, which indicates the agent's turn.

Then, for the next turn, 1 multiplied by minus 1 results in minus 1 to indicate the opponent's turn again.

See this code.

Alpha beta pruning, optimized by exploring the sensible paths only.

Alpha beta pruning is a technique used with the Minimax search algorithm to short-circuit exploring areas of the game tree that are known to produce poor solutions.

This technique optimizes the Minimax search algorithm to save computation because insignificant paths are ignored.

Suppose that you're using a map to find the fastest route home.

Route A takes 30 minutes.

Next, you check Route B.

The app tells you that the first segment of Route B involves a ferry ride that takes 45 minutes.

You stop calculating Route B immediately.

You don't need to know how fast the roads are after the ferry, because the first step alone is already longer than the entirety of route A, Route B is guaranteed to be worse.

You prune that route from your calculations.

Because we know how the Connect4 game tree explodes, we clearly see that ignoring more paths will improve computational performance significantly.

Figure 3.22.

The alpha-beta pruning algorithm works by storing the best score for the maximizing player and the best score for the minimizing player as alpha and beta, respectively.

Initially, alpha is set as negative infinity, and beta is set as infinity, the worst score for each player.

If the best score of the minimizing player is less than the best score of the maximizing player, it's logical that other child paths of the nodes already visited would not affect the best score.

Figure 3.22.

An example of alpha-beta pruning.

Figure 3.23 illustrates the changes made in the Minimax search flow to accommodate the optimization of alpha-beta pruning.

The shaded blocks are the additional steps in the Minimax search algorithm's flow.

Figure 3.23.

Flow of the Minimax search algorithm with alpha beta pruning.

Steps in Minimax search with alpha beta pruning.

The following steps are additions to the Minimax search algorithm that allow termination of exploration of paths when the best score found doesn't change the outcome.

1.

Is the current mode max?

Again, determine whether the algorithm is currently attempting to maximize or minimize the score.

2.

Is best n greater than or equal to alpha?

If the current mode is to maximize the score and the current best score, best n is greater than alpha, we found a better path than previously discovered.

We update alpha to reflect this new highest value.

This step tightens the search window, potentially helping us prune future branches, but we don't stop exploring this node yet, unless alpha becomes greater than or equal to beta in step 4.

3.

Set alpha as best n.

This step sets the variable alpha to best n.

4.

Is alpha greater than or equal to beta?

The score is as good as other scores found, and the rest of the exploration of that node can be ignored by breaking.

5.

Is best n less than or equal to beta?

If the current mode minimizes the score and the current best score, best n is less than beta, we've found a stronger move for the minimizing player.

We update beta to reflect this new lowest value.

This step tightens the search window from the top, helping us prune future branches if the maximizer's best option, alpha, meets this new limit.

6.

Set beta as best n.

This step sets the variable beta to best n.

7.

Is alpha greater than or equal to beta?

The score is as good as other scores found, and the rest of the exploration of that node can be ignored by breaking.

Python code sample for minimax search with alpha beta pruning.

The code for achieving alpha beta pruning is largely the same as the code for minimax search, with the addition of keeping track of the alpha and beta values and maintaining those values as the tree is traversed.

When minimum min is selected, the variable min or max is minus 1, and when maximum max is selected, the variable min or max is 1.

Successful implementations of chess, checkers, and other classic games have been created.

Games with perfect information are games that have no hidden information, or don't involve random chance.

Creating game-playing agents for turn-based games with imperfect information.

Unknown future options exist in these games, including poker.

And Scrabble.

4. 进化算法

第4章。

进化算法。

本章探讨进化算法的灵感来源。

用进化算法解决问题。

理解遗传算法的生命周期。

开发遗传算法来解决优化问题。

什么是进化？

地球上没有任何事物是一出现就完全成形的。

如今活着的每一种生物，都是数百万年间积累的细微变化所形成的一条长链的结果。

这意味着，每种生物体的生理和认知特征，都是该生物体为求生存而适应其环境的结果。

我们常误以为进化是一条从原始祖先到现代形态的清晰直线。

实际上，进化是混乱的，而且有分支。

后代并非父母的完美克隆体，它们继承了混合的基因，并带有微小的随机变化，即突变。

在任何时刻，一个物种都是变种的集合，而不是一个单一的、纯粹的类别。

只有当你将时间远距离拉远，比较数千代间的平均值时，才会看到巨大、明显的差异。

图4.1描绘了进化的真实情况。

图4.1。

线性人类进化观与真实人类进化进化既简单又疯狂。

变异不断产生，大多数变种会消失，而有些变种则蓬勃发展。

大自然本质上是在巨大的可能性空间中搜寻最适应的。

进化算法复制了这一逻辑，在计算中搜索巨大的解空间，生成多样的候选解，选择最优的，并在代数间迭代。

本章探讨如何有意识地利用这一计算概念来解决困难的优化问题。

查尔斯·达尔文提出了以自然选择为中心的进化论。

自然选择是指种群中更强壮的成员因更适应环境而更可能生存下来的概念。

这意味着它们繁殖得更多，从而将有利于后代生存的特性传递下去，所以这些个体可能比其祖先表现得更好。

一个经典的适应性进化例子是桦尺蛾。

桦尺蛾最初颜色较浅，这能很好地伪装以躲避捕食者，因为这种蛾子能融入环境中浅色的表面。

只有大约百分之二的蛾子种群颜色较深。

然而，在工业革命之后，大约百分之九十五的该物种都变成了深色变种。

一种解释是，浅色的蛾子不再能像以前那样融入那么多表面，因为污染使表面变黑，使得浅色蛾子更显眼，因此更容易成为捕食者的目标。

深色蛾子在融入深色表面方面有更大优势。

所以，它们存活得更久，繁殖得更多，它们的遗传信息也更广泛地传播给了继承者，子女，孙辈，等等。

在桦尺蛾中，发生显著变化的属性是颜色。

然而，这个属性并非神奇地改变。

要发生这种变化，深色蛾子的基因必须传给后代。

在其他自然进化的例子中，我们可能会在不同个体间看到比颜色更显著的变化。

但事实上，这些变化是受到许多代中较低层次的基因差异影响的。

图4.2。

桦尺蛾的进化进化涵盖了这样一个概念：在一个物种的种群中，生物体成对繁殖。

后代是父母基因的组合，但这些后代通过突变发生了微小的变化。

然后，后代成为种群的一部分。

然而，并非种群中的所有成员都能存活下来。

我们知道，疾病、受伤以及被捕食者吃掉都会导致个体死亡。

更能适应周围环境的个体存活的可能性更大，这种情况催生了"适者生存"这一术语。

基于达尔文的进化论，一个种群具有以下属性：变异。

种群中的个体具有不同的基因特性。

遗传。

孩子会从父母那里继承基因特性。

选择。

选择是一种衡量个体适应度的机制。

更强的个体有更高的生存可能性。

这些属性意味着在进化过程中会发生以下事情。

繁殖。

通常是种群中的两个个体进行繁殖，产生后代。

交叉和突变。

通过繁殖产生的后代包含了父母基因的混合，交叉，并且在它们的遗传密码中有微小的随机变化。

图4.3。

一个繁殖和突变的简单例子。

总之，进化是一个非凡而混乱的系统，它产生各种生命形式的变体，其中一些比其他的更适合、更适应其环境。

这个理论也适用于进化算法。

通过生成多样化的解并在许多代中收敛到性能更优的解，我们从生物进化中汲取的经验被用来寻找实际问题的最优解。

本章探讨进化算法，它们是解决难题的强大、但被低估的方法。

进化算法可以单独使用，也可以与人工神经网络等构造结合使用。

扎实掌握这一概念为解决新颖问题开启了许多可能性。

进化算法能用于解决的问题。

解决。

进化算法并非设计用来解决所有问题，但它们在解决优化问题方面很强大，这类问题的解决方案可能来自大量的排列组合或选择。

这类问题通常由许多可行的解组成，即有效的解，其中一些比其他的更优。

进化算法很强大，但计算成本很高。

如果你的问题有很多陷阱，即局部最优，单个搜索者可能会被困住，那就使用它们。

通过使用一个种群，你可以同时探索许多区域，并分享幸存者的最佳特性。

考虑一下背包问题，这是计算机科学中用来探索算法如何工作及其效率的经典问题。

在背包问题中，一个背包能容纳特定的最大重量。

有若干物品可放入背包，每个物品都有不同的重量和价值。

目标是尽可能多地把物品装入背包，使得总价值最大化，同时总重量不超过背包的限重。

在该问题的最简单变体中，物品的物理尺寸和维度被忽略。

举个简单的例子，根据表4.1中的问题说明，一个背包能容纳的总重量为9公斤，并且可以从8个重量和价值各异的物品中任意选择。

图4.4。

一个简单例子。

背包问题。

表4.1。

背包承重能力9公斤。

这个问题有二的四次方等于二百五十六种可能的解，包括但不限于以下几种。

解1。

包含物品1，4和6。

总重量为8公斤，总价值为8美元。

解2。

包含物品1，3和7。

总重量为9公斤，总价值为14美元。

图4.5。

解3。

包含物品2，3和6。

虽然总价值为15美元，但总重量是15公斤，超过了背包的容量。

图4.5。

简单背包问题例子的最优解显然，价值最高的解是解2。

现在不必太关心可能性数量是如何计算的，但要明白，随着潜在物品数量的增加，可能性会呈爆炸式增长。

虽然这个简单的例子可以手动解决，但背包问题可能有不同的重量约束、不同数量的物品，以及每个物品不同的重量和价值，随着变量增多，手动解决变得不可能。

同样，当变量数量增长时，尝试暴力尝试每一种物品组合计算成本会很高。

因此，我们寻找能高效找到满意解的算法。

我们将能找到的最好解定义为满意解，而不是最优解。

尽管有些算法试图找到背包问题的唯一真正最优解，但进化算法尝试寻找最优解，却不一定保证能找到它。

该算法会找到对于用例来说可接受的解。

一个基于眼前问题对可接受解的看法。

例如，对于一个关键任务健康系统，"足够好"的解可能不行，但对于一个歌曲推荐系统，它可能就是完美的。

现在考虑更大的数据集，没错，想象一个巨大的背包，在表4.2中，物品数量以及不同的重量和价值使得问题难以手动解决。

通过理解这个数据集的复杂性，你很容易明白为什么许多计算机科学算法是通过其在解决此类问题上的表现来衡量的。

你也明白了为什么算法是通过两个不同的指标来评判：计算效率，即运行速度有多快，以及解的适应度，即答案有多好。

在进化算法的背景下，我们非常关注适应度。

在背包问题中，产生更高总美元价值的解具有更高的适应度，无论计算机花多长时间找到它。

进化算法提供了找到背包问题解决方案的一种方法。

表4.2。

背包容量：6404180公斤。

解决这个问题的方法之一是使用暴力破解法。

这意味着计算每一种可能的物品组合，为所有可能的组合确定满足背包重量约束的每个组合的价值，然后选出最好的解。

图4.6显示了暴力破解法的一些基准分析数据。

该计算基于写作时一台普通个人电脑的硬件。

图4.6。

暴力破解背包问题的性能分析。

请记住背包问题。

我们将在本章通篇使用它，尝试理解、设计并开发一个遗传算法，来为这个问题找到可接受的解。

定义：从单个解的视角来看，性能是指该解解决问题的程度。

从算法的视角来看，性能可能是指某个特定配置在寻找解方面的表现。

最后，性能还可能指计算周期。

在计算机科学中，这个术语根据上下文有不同的用法。

使用遗传算法解决背包问题背后的思想，可以应用于一系列实际问题。

例如，如果一家物流公司想根据目的地来优化卡车的装载，遗传算法将会很有用。

如果同一家公司想找到多个目的地之间的最短路线，遗传算法再次会很有用。

如果一家工厂通过传送带系统将物品提炼成原材料，而物品的顺序会影响生产力，那么遗传算法对于确定该顺序将会很有用。

当你深入了解遗传算法的思想、方法和生命周期后，应该就能清楚在哪里可以应用这种算法，或许你也会想到新的用例。

重要的是要记住，遗传算法是随机的，在其整个生命周期中都依赖随机概率，例如生成随机的初始种群、随机选择父代或随机突变基因。

因此，即使输入相同，每次运行算法的输出也可能不同。

遗传算法的生命周期。

遗传算法是进化算法家族中的一种特定算法。

每种算法都基于相同的进化前提，但在生命周期的各部分有微小的调整，以满足不同问题的需求。

第5章探讨了其中的一些参数。

遗传算法用于评估大型搜索空间，以寻找一个好的解。

请记住，遗传算法不保证能找到最佳解，它试图在避免局部最优解的同时寻找最佳解，但它可能会停留在一个优秀但非全局最优的解上。

全局最优是指可能的最佳解，而局部最优则次之。

图4.7展示了如果要最小化结果，即值越小越好，可能的最佳解的情况。

如果目标是最大化一个解，那么值越大越好。

像遗传算法这样的优化算法，旨在逐步找到局部最优解，以寻找全局最优解。

图4.7。

局部最优与全局最优，在配置算法参数时要特别小心，以便它在开始时力求解决方案的多样性，并在每一代中朝向更好的解决方案靠拢。

在开始时，潜在的解在个体基因构成上应该有广泛的差异。

如果一开始没有差异，陷入局部最优的风险就会增加。

图4.8。

图4.8。

从多样性到收敛遗传算法的配置基于问题空间。

每个问题都有一个独特的领域，数据在其中以不同的方式表示和评估。

以下是遗传算法的一般生命周期。

1。

创建种群。

此步骤涉及生成一组初始的随机候选解。

2。

衡量种群中个体的适应度。

此步骤通过使用一个适应度函数来确定特定解的好坏，该函数对解评分以判断它们有多好。

3。

根据适应度选择父代。

此步骤涉及选择父代。

选择将产生后代的父代。

4。

从父代繁殖个体。

此步骤通过组合所选父代的基因并向后代引入随机突变，来生成新的候选解。

5。

填充下一代。

此步骤涉及从种群中选择将存活到下一代的个体和后代。

实现遗传算法涉及多个步骤。

这些步骤涵盖了算法生命周期的各个阶段。

图4.9。

遗传算法生命周期。

编码解空间。

我们该如何使用遗传算法来寻找背包问题的解？

本节回答这个问题。

当我们使用遗传算法时，正确执行编码步骤至关重要。

它需要仔细设计可能状态的表示方式。

状态是一种具有特定规则的数据结构，代表问题的可能解。

此外，一组状态构成了种群，图4.10。

图4.10。

编码解决方案。

染色体。

在进化算法中，单个候选解被称为染色体。

染色体由基因组成。

基因是该单元的逻辑类型，等位基因是存储在该单元中的实际值。

基因型是对一种解的表示，而表现型是一个独特的解本身。

如果将这些术语应用到烘焙中，基因型是食谱，表现型是从烤箱里出来的蛋糕，即结果，而适应度则是蛋糕的味道有多好。

每条染色体总是具有相同数量的基因。

一组染色体构成一个种群，如下图所示。

表示一个解种群的数据结构的术语在背包问题中，若干物品可以放入背包。

描述一个包含某些物品而排除其他物品的可能解的一个简单方法是二进制编码，图4.11。

二进制编码用零表示排除的物品，用一表示包含的物品。

例如，如果索引为3的基因处的值是1，则表示该物品被标记为包含。

完整的二进制字符串长度总是相同的，即可供选择的物品数量。

图4.11，背包问题的二进制编码二进制编码，用零和一表示可能的解。

二进制编码将基因表示为0或1，因此染色体由一串二进制位表示。

我们可以用多种方式使用二进制编码来表达特定元素的存在，甚至将数值编码为二进制数。

二进制编码的优点是对于离散过程非常高效。

问题，涉及"是"、"否"或"开-关"状态的决策。

因为二进制编码使用原始类型，它对内存的需求较小，并允许快速计算。

然而，这种方法并非万能。

如果你的问题涉及精确的十进制数字，比如优化的股票价格，或者特定的序列，比如地图中城市的顺序，二进制编码会使算法更难找到解。

在这些情况下，实值编码或排列编码是更好的选择。

与其他编码策略相比，二进制编码对工作内存的需求更小，并且根据所使用的编程语言，二进制操作在计算上更快。

但要运用批判性思维，确保编码对于眼前的问题有意义，能很好地表示解。

否则，算法可能会表现不佳。

图4.12显示了背包问题中的可能物品是如何进行二进制编码的。

图4.12。

背包问题更大数据集的二进制编码给定一个包含26个重量和价值各异的物品的数据集的背包问题，一个二进制字符串可以表示每个物品是否被包含。

结果是一个26个字符的字符串，其中对于每个索引，0表示相应的物品被排除，1表示相应的物品被包含。

其他编码方案，包括实值编码、顺序编码和树编码，将在第5章讨论。

练习。

因为可能的单词数量始终相同，并且单词始终在相同的位置，所以可以使用二进制编码来描述哪些单词被包含，哪些被排除。

该染色体由九个基因组成，每个基因表示词组中的一个单词。

请看此图。

创建解的种群。

起初，我们创建种群。

遗传算法的第一步是初始化随机的潜在解。

在初始化种群的过程中，虽然染色体是随机生成的，但必须考虑问题的约束条件，并且潜在的解应该是有效的，或者如果违反了约束，则被赋予一个极差的适应度分数，即惩罚。

一个更有效的技术是使用修复函数。

算法自动调整无效的解，例如从超重的背包中移除一个物品，使其有效，而不是丢弃它。

种群中随机就位的个体可能不能很好地解决问题，但解是有效的。

如前面打包物品进背包的例子所述，一个指定多次打包同一物品的解应该是无效的，并且不应该成为可能解的种群的一部分。

图4.13。

图4.13。

创建初始种群。

考虑到背包问题解的状态是如何表示的，这个实现随机决定每个物品是否应被放入包中。

也就是说，只有满足重量限制约束的解才应该被考虑。

简单地从左到右移动并随机选择是否包含物品的问题在于，这会对染色体左端的物品产生偏倚。

同样地，如果我们从右边开始，我们会偏向右端的物品。

解决这个问题的一个方法是生成一个带有随机基因的完整个体，然后确定该解是否有效且不违反任何约束。

为无效解分配极差的分数也能解决这个问题。

图4.14。

一个解的种群示例。

用于生成种群的Python代码示例。

要生成一个可能的解的初始种群，创建一个空数组来存放个体。

然后，对于种群中的每个个体，创建一个空数组来存放该个体的基因。

每个基因被随机设置为1或0，表示该基因索引处的物品是否被包含。

看这段代码？

衡量种群中个体的适应度。

创建种群后，你要衡量种群中每个个体的适应度。

适应度定义了解决方案的表现有多好。

适应度函数对遗传算法的生命周期至关重要。

如果个体的适应度被错误地衡量，或者以一种不力求达到最优解的方式衡量，那么选择新个体的父代和新世代的过程将会受到影响。

结果，算法就会有缺陷，无法努力找到可能的最佳解。

适应度函数类似于第3章探讨的启发式方法。

它们是寻找良好解的指南。

图4.15。

衡量个体的适应度。

在我们的例子中，该解尝试在遵守重量限制约束的同时最大化背包中物品的价值。

适应度函数衡量每个个体背包中物品的总价值。

结果是，总价值更高的个体适应度更高。

一个无效的个体出现在图4.16中，所以它的适应度得分是0，一个极差的分数。

它超过了这个问题实例的重量容量，即6404180。

图4.16。

个体染色体及其表现。

我们故意将此分数压低到0，以作为一种严厉的惩罚。

即使那个背包可能装着价值一百万美元的物品，它太重而无法携带的事实使其实际上毫无价值。

零分教会了算法。

违反规则会导致失败，无论里面的东西多么诱人。

根据所解决的问题，我们可能必须最小化或最大化适应度函数的结果。

在背包问题中，我们可以在约束条件下最大化背包的装载物，或者我们可以最小化背包中的空闲空间。

我们的方法取决于我们对问题的解读。

用于计算适应度的Python代码示例。

要计算背包问题中一个个体的适应度，我们必须确定该个体所包含物品的价值总和。

我们通过将总价值设为零，然后遍历每个基因来确定其代表的物品是否被包含来完成此任务。

如果物品被包含，则该基因所代表物品的价值被加到总价值中。

同样地，我们计算总重量以确保解是有效的。

计算适应度和检查约束的概念可以分开，以实现更清晰的关注点分离。

看这段代码。

根据适应度选择父代。

遗传算法的下一步是选择将产生新个体的父代。

在达尔文的理论中，更适应的个体比其他个体有更高的繁殖可能性，因为它们通常活得更久。

此外，这些个体由于在环境中表现优越，而包含了值得继承的理想属性。

也就是说，有些个体即使不是整个群体中最适应的，也有可能繁殖，而这些个体可能包含强大的特性，尽管它们整体上并不强大。

每个个体都有一个计算出的适应度，用来确定它被选为新个体父代的概率。

这个属性增加了遗传算法的随机性。

图4.17。

选择父代。

根据适应度选择父代的一种流行技术是轮盘赌选择。

这种策略根据个体的适应度给予它们轮盘上不同的扇区。

轮盘旋转，然后选择一个个体。

更高的适应度让个体获得轮盘上更大的一块。

重复此过程，直到达到所需的父代数量。

通过计算16个不同适应度个体的概率，轮盘为每个个体分配了一个扇区。

由于许多个体表现相似，会有许多大小相似的扇区。

图4.18。

图4.18。

确定每个个体的选择概率。

选择用于繁殖新后代的父代数量，由所需的后代总数决定，而所需的后代总数又由每一代期望的种群规模决定。

两个父代被选中，然后产生后代。

这个过程在选择不同父代的情况下重复，选中的个体有可能不再被选中。

个体会多次成为父代，直到生成所需数量的后代。

两个父代可以繁殖一个混合孩子或两个混合孩子。

这个概念稍后会在"从父代繁殖个体"部分更清晰地阐述。

记住，在背包问题的例子中，适应度更高的个体是那些在遵守重量限制约束的同时，以最高综合价值装满背包的个体。

使用种群模型。

种群模型控制种群的多样性。

稳态和代际是两种种群模型，是设计算法配置的总体思想。

稳态模型每一代只替换种群的一部分。

这种种群管理的高层次方法，并非其他选择策略的替代方案，而是一个使用这些策略的体系。

其思想是，保留种群中的大多数成员，并移除一小部分较弱的个体，用新的后代来替换。

这个过程模拟了生与死的循环，其中弱小的个体死亡，新的个体通过繁殖产生。

如果种群中有100个个体，那么其中一部分是现有的个体，较小的一部分是通过繁殖产生的新个体。

当前一代可能有80个现有个体和20个新个体。

代际模型每一代替换整个种群。

这种种群管理的高层次方法类似于稳态模型，但并非选择策略的替代方案。

代际模型创建与种群规模数量相等的后代个体，并用新的后代替换整个种群。

如果种群中有100个个体，那么每一代将通过繁殖产生100个新个体。

稳态和代际是设计算法配置的总体思想。

执行轮盘赌选择。

适应度得分较高的染色体更可能被选中，但适应度得分较低的染色体仍有小概率被选中，因为尽管它们整体不强，但可能拥有对最优解有益的强大单个基因。

术语“轮盘赌选择”源于赌场里的轮盘赌，轮盘被分成若干扇区。

通常，轮盘旋转，一颗弹珠被释放到轮盘中。

选中的扇区就是轮盘停止转动时弹珠落在的那个扇区。

在这个类比中，染色体被分配到轮盘的各个扇区。

适应度得分高的染色体获得轮盘上更大的扇区，适应度得分低的染色体获得较小的扇区。

染色体被随机选中，就像轮盘赌中的弹珠随机落在一个扇区上。

这个类比是概率选择的一个例子。

每个个体都有被选中的机会，无论机会是低还是高。

个体的选择机会影响种群的多样性以及本章前面提到的收敛速度。

轮盘赌选择的Python代码示例。

首先，我们确定每个个体被选择的概率，通过将个体的适应度除以种群的总适应度来计算每个个体的概率。

我们可以使用轮盘赌选择来实现这一目的，旋转轮盘，直到选中所需数量的个体。

对于每次选择，会计算一个介于0和1之间的随机十进制数。

如果某个个体的适应度落在这个概率范围内，该个体就被选中。

我们可以使用其他概率方法来确定每个个体的概率，包括标准差，即把个体的值与群体的平均值进行比较。

看这段代码？

从父代繁殖个体。

当父代被选定后，就必须进行繁殖，以从父代产生新的后代。

通常，从两个父代产生子代涉及两个步骤。

第一个概念是交叉，即将第一个父代染色体的一部分与第二个父代染色体的一部分混合，反之亦然。

这个过程会产生两个后代，它们包含了父母基因的反向混合。

第二个概念是突变，这意味着随机而轻微地改变后代，以在种群中创造变异。

定义：交叉是指混合两个个体的基因，以创造一个或多个后代个体。

交叉的灵感来源于繁殖的概念。

后代个体根据交叉策略，部分继承自他们的父代。

交叉策略在很大程度上受编码方式的影响。

图4.19。

繁殖后代。

单点交叉，从每个父代各继承一部分。

在单点交叉中，先在染色体结构中选择一个点。

然后，参照两个亲代，使用第一个亲代的前部分和第二个亲代的后部分。

这两部分组合成一个新的后代。

第二个后代可以通过使用第二个亲代的前部分和第一个亲代的后部分来生成。

单点交叉适用于二进制编码、顺序排列编码和实值编码。

图4.20，单点交叉。

交叉的Python代码示例。

为了创建两个新的后代个体，创建一个空数组来存放新个体。

将父代A从索引0到所需索引的所有基因，与父代B从所需索引到染色体末端的所有基因拼接起来，创建一个后代个体。

反向操作则创建第二个后代个体。

看这段代码。

两点交叉，从每个父代继承更多部分。

在两点交叉中，在染色体结构中选择两个点。

然后，参照两个亲代，以交替方式选取各部分，组成一个完整的后代个体。

这个过程与我们刚讨论过的单点交叉类似。

完整地描述这个过程：后代由第一个亲代的第一部分、第二个亲代的第二部分和第一个亲代的第三部分组成。

可以将两点交叉理解为拼接数组以创建新数组。

再次，第二个个体可以通过使用每个亲代的相反部分来生成。

两点交叉适用于二进制编码和实值编码，图4.21。

图4.21两点交叉。

均匀交叉，从每个父代继承许多部分。

在均匀交叉中，这比两点交叉更进一步，会创建一个掩码来表示每个父代的哪些基因将被用于生成后代。

反向过程可用于生成第二个后代，并且每次创建后代时都可以随机生成掩码，以最大化多样性。

一般来说，均匀交叉会产生更多样化的个体，因为后代的属性与父代有很大不同。

均匀交叉适用于二进制编码和实值编码，图4.22。

图4.22。

均匀交叉。

突变。

突变涉及轻微改变单个后代，以促进种群多样性。

根据问题的性质和编码方法，会使用几种不同的突变方法。

突变中的一个参数是突变率，即后代染色体发生突变的可能性。

就像在生物体中一样，有些染色体的突变会多于其他染色体。

后代并非其亲代染色体的精确组合，而是包含微小的遗传差异。

突变对于促进种群多样性和防止算法陷入局部最优解至关重要。

高突变率意味着个体有很大可能被选中进行突变，或者根据突变策略，个体染色体中的基因有很大可能发生突变。

高突变意味着更多样性，但过多的多样性可能导致优良解的退化。

练习：均匀交叉会为这些染色体产生什么样的结果？

在位串突变中，二进制编码染色体中的一个基因被随机选择，并更改为另一个有效值。

图4.23。

当使用非二进制编码时，会适用其他突变机制。

用于二进制编码的翻转位突变。

在翻转位突变中，二进制编码染色体中的所有基因都被反转为相反的值。

原来是1的位置变成0，原来是0的位置变成1。

这种突变可能会严重降低表现优良的解的质量，通常只在必须不断向种群引入多样性时使用。

图4.24。

图4.24。

翻转位突变。

填充下一代。

当种群中个体的适应度被衡量完毕，且后代已被繁殖出来后，下一步是选择哪些个体存活到下一代。

种群的规模通常是固定的，而由于通过繁殖引入了更多个体，一些个体必须死亡并从种群中移除。

图4.25图4.25。

填充下一代。

选取适合种群规模的最优个体并淘汰其余个体，这似乎是个好主意。

这种策略称为精英主义，但如果存活的个体在基因构成上相似，精英主义可能会造成个体多样性的停滞。

本节提到的选择策略可以用来确定哪些个体被选为下一代种群的一部分。

探索与开发。

运行遗传算法总是要在探索和开发之间取得平衡。

理想的情况是，个体具有多样性，且种群作为一个整体在搜索空间中寻找截然不同的潜在解。

然后，利用较强的局部解空间来找到最满意的解。

假设你被空降到一片雾气弥漫的山脉中，需要找到最高的山峰。

你可以采用探索或开发策略。

探索，即突变，就像滑翔伞降落到一个完全随机的新地点。

你可能落在一个山谷里，也可能发现一片你之前无法看到的巨大山脉。

开发，即交叉选择，就像从你当前站的位置向山上攀登。

你保证会走得更高，但如果你探索不够，你可能会被困在一个小山丘上，即一个局部最大值，而错过几英里外珠穆朗玛峰，即全局最大值。

这种情况的美妙之处在于，随着个体的进化，该算法在尽可能多地探索搜索空间的同时，也在开发强大的解。

当个体被选择并且下一代被填充后，所有个体的适应度会再次被衡量。

图4.26。

图4.26。

再次衡量个体的适应度。

停止条件。

由于遗传算法在寻找更好解的过程中是逐代迭代的，因此需要一个停止条件。

否则，算法可能会永远运行下去。

停止条件是算法结束时满足的条件。

那一代种群中最强的个体被选为最佳解。

最简单的停止条件是固定的代数，一个指定算法将运行多少代的常量值。

另一种方法是当达到某个特定适应度时停止。

当已知期望的最小适应度，但解未知时，这种方法很有用。

停滞是进化算法中的一个问题，即种群连续几代都产出强度相似的解。

如果一个种群停滞了，未来几代产生强解的可能性就很低。

一个停止条件可以观察每一代中最佳个体适应度的变化，如果适应度变化甚微，就选择停止算法。

运行遗传算法的Python代码示例。

遗传算法的各个步骤在一个主函数中使用，该函数概述了整个生命周期。

可变参数包括种群规模、算法运行的代数、适应度函数中使用的背包容量，以及用于交叉和突变步骤的可变交叉位置和突变率。

看这段代码？

正如本章开头所提到的，背包问题可以用暴力破解法解决，这需要生成并分析超过六千万种组合。

当比较旨在解决同一问题的遗传算法时，如果探索和开发的参数配置得当，我们可以看到计算效率要高得多。

遗传算法在三秒内找到了最优解，而暴力破解法花费了七分钟。

请记住，在某些情况下，遗传算法产生的是一个足够好的解，它未必是可能的最佳解，但是是令人满意的。

再次强调，是否针对某个问题使用遗传算法取决于具体情境。

图4.27。

暴力破解性能与遗传算法性能对比。

配置遗传算法的参数。

在设计及配置遗传算法时，我们必须做出若干影响算法性能的决定。

性能的关注点分为两个领域。

算法应努力在找到问题的良好解方面表现优异，并且从计算角度看，应高效运行。

如果设计出的遗传算法在计算上比其它传统技术更昂贵，那么用它来解决问题就毫无意义了。

所使用的编码方法、适应度函数以及其他算法参数，都会影响在获得良好解和计算效率这两方面的性能。

以下是需要考虑的一些参数。

染色体编码。

染色体编码方法需要仔细考虑，以确保它适用于该问题，并且潜在的解能努力达到全局最大值。

编码方案是算法成功的核心。

种群规模。

种群规模是可配置的。

更大的种群能促进可能解的多样性。

然而，更大的种群在每一代都需要更多计算。

有时，一个较大的种群能平衡对突变的需求，突变在开始时带来多样性，但在代际过程中则不提供多样性。

一种有效的方法是，从较小的种群开始，再根据表现来扩大规模。

种群初始化。

虽然种群中的个体是随机初始化的，但确保解的有效性对于优化遗传算法的计算以及用正确的约束初始化个体至关重要。

这可以节省不必要的计算周期。

后代数量：每一代产生的后代数量是可以配置的。

考虑到繁殖后，种群的一部分会被淘汰以确保种群规模固定，更多的后代意味着更多样性，但也有可能为了容纳这些后代而淘汰掉优良解的风险。

如果种群是动态的，种群规模可能每代都会改变，但这种方法需要更多参数来配置和控制。

父代选择方法。

用于选择父代的方法可以根据问题以及预期的探索与开发能力来配置。

交叉方法。

交叉方法与所使用的编码方法相关，但可以进行配置以鼓励或抑制种群的多样性。

产生的后代个体仍必须得到一个有效的解。

突变率。

突变率是另一个可配置的参数，它能在后代和潜在解中引入更多多样性。

更高的突变率意味着更多样性，但过多的多样性可能会使表现优良的个体退化。

突变率可以随时间变化，在早期代数中产生更多多样性，而在后期代数中减少多样性。

突变方法。

突变方法与交叉方法类似，也取决于所使用的编码方法。

突变方法的一个重要属性是，在修改之后，仍必须产生一个有效的解。

否则，它会被赋予一个极差的适应度分数。

代际选择方法。

与用于选择父代的选择方法类似，代际选择方法必须选择能存活过这一代的个体。

根据所使用的选择方法，算法可能收敛得过快而停滞，或者探索得太久。

停止条件。

算法的停止条件必须基于问题和期望的结果而具有意义。

计算复杂度和时间是停止条件主要的关注点。

进化算法的用例。

进化算法有广泛的用途。

有些算法解决孤立的问题，另一些则将进化算法与其他技术结合，创造出解决难题的新颖方法，例如：预测投资者在股市中的行为。

投资的消费者每天都在做出决定，是买入更多特定股票，持有手中的股票，还是卖出。

这些行为序列可以经过进化，并映射到投资者投资组合的结果上。

金融机构可以利用这种洞察，主动提供有价值的客户服务和指导。

在机器学习中选择特征。

机器学习将在第8章讨论，但其一个基本概念涉及使用特定属性，即特征，来对某物进行分类。

如果我们观察房屋，可能会发现许多与房屋相关的属性，比如房龄、建筑材料、面积、颜色和位置。

但要预测市场价值，也许只有房龄、面积和位置才重要。

遗传算法可以揭示出最重要的那些孤立特征。

破解代码和密码。

密码是一种以特定方式编码以看似别物的消息，通常用于隐藏信息。

如果接收者不知道如何解译该消息，就无法理解消息。

进化算法可以生成许多改变加密消息以揭示原始消息的可能性。

第5章将深入探讨适应不同问题空间的遗传算法的高级概念。

我们会探索编码、交叉、突变和选择的技术，并揭示有效的替代方案。

进化算法总结。
