英文原文

Chapter 6.

Swarm Intelligence Ants In this chapter, seeing and understanding the inspiration for swarm intelligence algorithms.

Solving problems with swarm intelligence algorithms.

Designing and implementing an ant colony optimization algorithm.

What is swarm intelligence?

Swarm intelligence algorithms, much like the evolutionary algorithms discussed in chapter 5, are a family of nature-inspired algorithms.

Whereas evolutionary algorithms mimic genetic reproduction, swarm intelligence mimics the collective behavior of animals.

When we observe the world around us, we see many life forms that are seemingly primitive and unintelligent as individuals, yet exhibit intelligent emergent behavior when acting in groups.

An example of these life forms is the ant.

A single ant can carry 10 to 50 times its own body weight and run 700 times its body length per minute.

These qualities are impressive, but when acting in a group, that single ant can accomplish much more.

In a group, ants can build colonies, find and retrieve food, warn and recognize other ants, and use peer pressure to influence other members of the colony.

They achieve these tasks through pheromones, essentially perfumes that ants drop as they move.

Other ants sense these perfumes and change their behavior based on them.

Ants have access to 10 to 20 types of pheromones that communicate different intentions.

Because individual ants use pheromones to indicate their intentions and needs, we observe complex emergent intelligent behavior when they act in groups.

Figure 6.1 shows ants working as a team to create a bridge between two points to enable other ants to carry out tasks.

These tasks may be to retrieve food or materials for their colony.

Figure 6.1.

A group of ants working together to cross a chasm.

An experiment based on real-life harvesting ants showed that they always converged on the shortest path between the nest and the food source.

Figure 6.2 depicts the difference in colony movement from the start to the time when ants have walked their paths and increased the pheromone intensity on those paths.

This outcome was observed in a classical asymmetric bridge experiment with real ants.

Notice that the ants converged on the shortest path after eight minutes.

Figure 6.2 Asymmetric Bridge Experiment Why does the shorter bridge win?

The answer isn't magic, it's speed.

An ant on the short bridge can make two trips in the time an ant on the long bridge makes only one.

Because the ants on the short bridge make twice as many trips, they lay down twice as much pheromone per minute, creating a snowball effect that pulls the rest of the colony toward the short path.

Ant colony optimization algorithms simulate the emergent behavior shown in this experiment.

In the case of finding the shortest path, the algorithm converges on a similar state, as observed with real ants.

Swarm intelligence algorithms are powerful tools for solving optimization problems when the search space is vast and rugged, and finding an absolute best solution is mathematically difficult.

These problems belong to the same broad class that genetic algorithms aim to solve, but the choice between the two types of algorithms often comes down to how the problem is encoded.

Genetic algorithms typically are better for discrete choices, such as choosing which items to pack in a knapsack.

Swarm algorithms, including particle swarm optimization, PSO, excel at solving continuous numeric problems, such as tuning the exact floating point weights of a neural network.

We dive into the technicalities of solving optimization problems with PSO in chapter 7.

Swarm intelligence is useful in several real-world contexts, some of which are represented in Figure 6.3.

Figure 6.3 Problems Addressed by Swarm Optimization The following sections explore specific implementations inspired by these concepts.

The ACO algorithm is inspired by the behavior of ants moving between destinations, dropping pheromones, and acting on pheromones they come across.

The emergent behavior is ants converging on paths of least resistance.

Problems that ACO algorithms can solve.

Suppose we're visiting a carnival that has many attractions.

The attractions are located in different areas, with varying distances among them.

Because we don't feel like wasting time by walking too much, we'll attempt to find the shortest paths between the attractions.

Figure 6.4 illustrates the attractions at a small carnival and the distances among them.

Different paths to the attractions have different total lengths of travel.

Figure 6.4.

Carnival attractions and the paths between them.

The figure shows six attractions to visit, with 15 paths between them.

This illustration may look familiar.

The problem is represented by a fully connected graph, described in chapter 2.

The attractions are nodes or vertices, and the paths between attractions are edges.

We use the following formula to calculate the number of edges in a fully connected graph.

As the number of attractions gets larger, the number of edges explodes.

N times open parenthesis n minus 1, close parenthesis, divided by 2.

Attractions have different distances between them.

Figure 6.5 depicts the distance on each path between attractions.

It also shows a possible path between attractions.

The lines in Figure 6.5 showing distances between attractions are not drawn to the scale of the distance.

Figure 6.5.

Distances between attractions and a possible path to all attractions.

If we spend some time analyzing the distances between all the attractions, we find that Figure 6.6 shows an optimal path between all attractions, solving the carnival problem.

We visit the attractions in this sequence, swings, ferris wheel, circus, carousel, balloons, and bumper cars.

Figure 6.6, distances between attractions and an optimal path.

The small data set with six attractions is trivial to solve by hand, but if we increase the number of attractions to 15, the number of possibilities increases substantially.

Figure 6.7.

Suppose that the attractions are servers and the paths are network connections.

We need intelligent algorithms to solve these types of problems.

Figure 6.7, a larger data set of attractions and the paths between them.

Exercise.

Find the shortest path in this carnival configuration by hand.

See this figure.

Solution.

See this figure.

One way to solve this problem computationally is to brute force it.

Every combination of tours, a tour is a sequence of visits in which every attraction is visited once, of the attractions is generated and evaluated.

Then the best tour is picked.

Again, this solution may seem reasonable, but in a large data set, the computation is expensive and time consuming.

A brute force approach with 48 attractions runs for tens of hours before completing.

Representing state, what do paths and ants look like?

Given the carnival problem, we need to represent the data in a way that the ACO algorithm can process.

Because we have several attractions and all the distances between them, we can use a distance matrix to represent the problem space simply and accurately.

A distance matrix is a 2D array in which every index represents an entity.

The related set is the distance between that entity and another entity.

Similarly, each index in the list denotes a unique entity.

This matrix, figure 6.8 and table 6.1, is similar to the adjacency matrix in chapter 2.

Figure 6.8, an example carnival problem.

Table 6.1 distances in meters between attractions.

Python code sample for storing attraction distances.

The distances between attractions can be represented as a distance matrix, an array of arrays in which a reference to xy in the array references the distance between attractions x and y.

The distance between the same attraction is zero because it's in the same position.

We can also create this array programmatically by iterating through data from a file and creating each element.

See this code.

The next element to represent is the ants.

Ants move to different attractions and leave pheromones behind.

Ants also make a judgment about which attraction to visit next.

Finally, ants know their own total distance traveled.

are the basic properties of an ant.

Figure 6.9 Memory.

In the ACO algorithm, memory is the list of attractions already visited.

Best fitness.

This property is the shortest total distance traveled across attractions.

Action.

Choose the next destination to visit and drop pheromones along the way.

Figure 6.9.

Properties of an ant.

Python code sample for ant data and operations.

Although the abstract concept of an ant entails memory, best fitness, and action, specific data and functions are required to solve the carnival problem.

To encapsulate the logic for an ant, we can use a class.

When an instance of the ant class is initialized, an empty array is initialized to represent a list of attractions that the ant will visit.

Furthermore, a random attraction is selected to be the starting point for that specific ant.

See this code?

The ant class also contains several functions used for ant movement.

The visit functions determine which attraction the ant moves to next.

The visit attraction function generates a random chance of visiting a random attraction.

In this case, visit random attraction is called.

Otherwise, roulette wheel selection is used with a calculated list of probabilities.

For more details, see choose the next visit for each ant in the next section.

See this code?

Last, the get distance traveled function calculates the total distance traveled by a specific ant using its list of visited attractions.

This distance must be minimized to find the shortest path and is used as the fitness for the ants.

See this code.

The final data structure to design is pheromone trails.

Like distances between attractions, pheromone intensity on each path can be represented as a distance matrix, but instead of containing distances, the matrix contains pheromone intensities.

In figure 6.10, thicker lines indicate more intense pheromone trails.

Table 6.2 describes the pheromone trails between attractions.

Example Pheromone Intensity on Paths.

Table 6.2.

Pheromone Intensity Between Attractions.

The ACO algorithm lifecycle.

Now that we understand the data structures required, we can dive into the workings of the ACO algorithm.

The approach in designing an ACO algorithm is based on the problem space being addressed.

Each problem has a unique context and a different domain in which data is represented, but the principles remain the same.

That said, let's look into how we can configure an ACO algorithm to solve the carnival problem.

The general lifecycle of such an algorithm is as follows, illustrated in Figure 6.11.

1.

Set up the pheromones.

Create the concept of pheromone trails between attractions and initialize their intensity values.

2.

Set up the population of ants.

Create a population of ants in which each ant starts at a different attraction.

3.

Choose the next visit for each ant.

Choose the next attraction to visit for each ant until each ant has visited all attractions once.

4.

Check for more destinations.

Verify that the ant has visited all attractions.

If not, the ant chooses another visit, repeating step 3.

Otherwise, the tour is complete.

5.

Update the pheromone trails.

Update the intensity of pheromone trails based on the ants' movements on them, and factor in the evaporation of pheromones.

6.

Update the best solution.

Update the best solution given the total distance covered by each ant.

7.

Determine the stopping condition.

The process of ants visiting attractions repeats for several iterations.

One iteration is every ant visiting all attractions once.

The stopping criterion determines the total number of iterations to run.

More iterations allow ants to make better decisions based on the pheromone trails.

8.

Return the best solution.

Output the best path found after the stopping condition is met.

Figure 6.11.

The ACO algorithm lifecycle.

Set up pheromones.

The first step in the ACO algorithm is initializing the pheromone trails.

Because no ants have walked on the paths between attractions yet, the pheromone trails will be initialized to 1.

When we set all pheromone trails to 1, no trail has any advantage over the others.

The important aspect is defining a reliable data structure to contain the pheromone trails, which we look at next.

Figure 6.12.

Figure 6.12.

Set up the pheromones.

This concept can be applied to other problems in which the pheromone intensity is defined by a heuristic other than distances between locations.

In Figure 6.13, the heuristic is the distance between two destinations.

Figure 6.13.

Initialization of pheromones.

Python code sample for initializing pheromones.

Like the attraction distances, the pheromone trails can be represented by a distance matrix, but referencing xy in this array provides the pheromone intensity on the path between attractions x and y.

The initial pheromone intensity on every path is initialized to 1.

Values for all paths should initialize with the same number to prevent biasing any paths from the start.

See this code?

Set up the population of ants.

The next step of the ACO algorithm is creating a population of ants that moves between the attractions and leaves pheromone trails.

Ants start at randomly assigned attractions, figure six point fifteen, at a random point in a potential sequence, because the ACO algorithm can be applied to problems in which actual distance doesn't exist.

After touring all the destinations, ants are set to their respective starting points.

6.14?

Python code sample for setting up ants.

We can adapt this principle to a different problem.

In a task scheduling problem, each ant starts at a different task.

Setting up the colony of ants includes initializing several ants and appending them to a list where they can be referenced later.

Remember that the initialization function of the ant class chooses a random attraction to start at.

See this code.

Choose the next visit for each ant.

Ants need to select the next attraction to visit.

They visit new attractions until they've visited all attractions once, which is a tour.

Ants choose the next destination based on two factors, figure 6.16.

Pheromone intensities, the pheromone intensity on all available paths.

Heuristic value: a result from a defined heuristic for all available paths, which is the distance of the path between attractions in the carnival problem.

Figure 6.16.

Choose the next visit for each ant.

Ants won't travel to destinations they've already visited.

If an ant has already visited the bumper cars, it won't travel to that attraction again in the current tour.

Simulating the random nature of ants.

The ACO algorithm has an element of randomness.

The intention is to allow ants the possibility of exploring less optimal immediate paths, which might result in a better overall tour distance.

In standard ACO, ants don't simply flip a coin to decide whether to explore.

Instead, they make a probabilistic decision based on two factors: pheromone strength, how popular the path is.

Distance heuristic, how short the path looks.

Think of the selection process as being like a roulette wheel.

A path with strong pheromones and a short distance gets a huge slice of the wheel.

A path with weak pheromones gets a tiny slice.

The ant spins the wheel to choose its next step.

The ant is likely to pick the best path, but sometimes the marble lands on the tiny slice, allowing the ant to explore new routes naturally.

Selecting a destination based on a heuristic.

When an ant faces the task of choosing the next non-random destination, it determines the pheromone intensity on that path and the heuristic value by using the following formula.

See this formula.

After it applies this function to every possible path to its respective destination, the ant selects the destination with the best overall value.

Figure 6.17 illustrates the possible paths from the circus, with respective distances and pheromone intensities.

In Figure 6.18, we work through the formula to demystify the calculations and show how the results affect decision-making.

Figure 6.17.

Possible Paths from the Circus.

Figure 6.18.

The Pheromone Influence and Heuristic Influence of the Formula.

The variables alpha, A, and beta B are used to give greater weight to the pheromone influence, or the heuristic influence.

These variables can be adjusted to balance the ant's judgment between making a move based on what it knows versus pheromone trails, which represent what the colony knows about that path.

These parameters are defined up front and usually aren't adjusted while the algorithm runs.

Think of the ant as a tourist trying to pick an attraction.

Pheromone, A, is social pressure.

Look at that long line of people, that ride must be amazing.

This is trusting the collective wisdom of the colony.

Heuristic B is greedy logic.

But that other ride is right next door, and I'm bored right now.

This is trusting the immediate distance.

If A is too high, the ants blindly follow the crowd even if it's long.

If B is too high, the ants become greedy loners and ignore the collective wisdom, turning the algorithm into a simple greedy search.

The following example works through each path starting at the circus and calculates the probabilities of moving to each respective attraction.

A is set to 1.

B is set to 2.

Because B is greater than A, the heuristic influence is favored in this example.

Let's work through an example of the calculations used to determine the probability of choosing a specific path.

Python code sample for ant decision making.

The code for calculating the probabilities of visiting the possible attractions is closely aligned with the mathematical functions that we've worked through.

Following are some interesting aspects of this implementation.

Determining the available attractions to visit.

Because the ant would have visited several attractions, it shouldn't return to those attractions.

The possible attractions array stores this value by removing visited attractions from the complete list of attractions, all attractions.

Using three variables to store the outcome of the probability calculations, possible indexes stores the attraction indexes, possible probabilities stores the probabilities for the respective index, and total probabilities stores the sum of all probabilities, which should equal one when the function is complete.

These three data structures could be represented by a class for a cleaner code convention.

See this code?

We meet roulette wheel selection again.

The roulette wheel selection function takes the possible probabilities and attraction indexes as input.

It generates a list of slices, each of which includes the index of the attraction in element 0, the start of the slice in index 1, and the end of the slice in index 2.

All slices contain a start and end between 0 and 1.

A random number between 0 and 1 is generated, and the slice into which it falls is selected as the winner.

See this code?

Now that we have probabilities of selecting the different attractions to visit, we'll use roulette wheel selection.

To recap, roulette wheel selection, discussed in chapters 3 and 4, gives different possibilities to portions of a wheel based on their fitness.

Then the wheel is spun, and an individual is selected.

A higher fitness gives an individual a larger slice of the wheel, as shown in figure 6.23 earlier in this chapter.

The process of choosing an attraction and visiting it continues for every ant until each ant has visited all the attractions once.

Update the pheromone trails.

Now that the ants have completed a tour of the attractions, all of them have left pheromones behind, changing the pheromone trails between the attractions.

Figure 6.24.

Two steps are involved in updating the pheromone trails, evaporation and depositing new pheromones.

Figure 6.24.

Update the pheromone trails.

Updating pheromones due to evaporation.

The concept of evaporation is also inspired by nature.

Over time, the pheromone trails lose their intensity.

Pheromones are updated by multiplying their respective current values by an evaporation factor, a parameter that can be adjusted to tweak the performance of the algorithm in terms of exploration and exploitation.

Why do pheromones need to evaporate?

Suppose that they didn't.

The very first path found, even if it was a terrible long route, would accumulate pheromones.

Without evaporation, that initial bad advice would stay forever, confusing future ants.

Evaporation acts like a validity timer, ensuring that old information disappears unless it is constant.

Reinforced by new ants, verifying that the path is still the best one.

Figure 6.25 illustrates the updated pheromone trails due to evaporation.

Figure 6.25.

Example of updating pheromone trails for evaporation.

Updating pheromones based on ant tours.

Pheromones are updated based on the ants that have moved along the paths.

If more ants move on a specific path, there will be more pheromones on that path.

Each ant contributes its fitness value to the pheromones on every path it moves on.

The effect is that ants with better solutions have greater influence on the best paths.

Figure 6.26 illustrates the updated pheromone trails based on ant movements.

Figure 6.26.

Pheromone updates based on ant movements.

Solution.

See this figure.

Python code sample for updating pheromones.

The update pheromones function applies two important concepts to the pheromone trails.

First, the current pheromone intensity is evaporated based on the evaporation rate.

If the evaporation rate is 0.5, for example, the intensity decreases by half.

The second operation adds pheromones based on ant movements on that path.

The amount of pheromones contributed by each ant is determined by the ant's fitness, which in this case is each respective ant's total distance traveled.

See this code.

Update the best solution.

The best solution is described by the sequence of attraction visits that has the lowest total distance.

Figure 6.27 illustrates this sequence.

Figure 6.27.

Update the best solution.

Python code sample for choosing the best solution.

After an iteration, after every ant completes a tour, a tour is complete when an ant visits every attraction, the best ant in the colony must be determined.

To make this determination, we find the ant that has the lowest total distance traveled and set it as the new best ant in the colony.

See this code.

Determine the stopping criteria.

The algorithm stops after several iterations, conceptually, the number of tours that the group of ants concludes.

If we have 10 iterations, each ant makes 10 tours.

That is, each ant visits each attraction once and does that 10 times.

Figure 6.28.

Figure 6.28.

Has the stopping condition been reached?

The stopping criteria for the ACO algorithm can differ based on the domain of the problem being solved.

In some cases, realistic limits are known, and when they're unknown, the following options are available.

Stop when a predefined number of iterations is reached.

In this scenario, we define a total number.

Of iterations for which the algorithm will always run.

If we define 100 iterations, each ant completes 100 tours before the algorithm terminates.

Stop when the best solution stagnates.

In this scenario, the best solution after each iteration is compared with the previous best solution.

If the solution doesn't improve after a defined number of iterations, the algorithm terminates.

If iteration 20 resulted in a solution with fitness 100 and that iteration is repeated until iteration 30, it's likely, but not guaranteed, that no better solution exists.

Python code sample for running the ACO algorithm.

The solve function ties everything together and should give you a better idea of the sequence of operations and the overall life cycle of the algorithm.

Notice that the algorithm runs for several defined total iterations.

Also, the ant colony is initialized to its starting point at the beginning of each iteration, and a new best ant is determined after each iteration.

See this code.

We can tweak several parameters to alter the exploration and exploitation of the ACO algorithm.

These parameters influence how long the algorithm takes to find a good solution.

Some randomness is good for exploring.

Balancing the weighting between heuristics and pheromones influences whether ants attempt a greedy search, when favoring heuristics, or trust pheromones more.

The evaporation rate also influences this balance.

The number of ants and the total number of iterations they have influences the quality of a solution.

When we add more ants and more iterations, more computation is required.

Based on the problem at hand, time to compute may influence these parameters.

Figure 6.29.

Figure 6.29.

Parameters that can be tweaked in the ACO algorithm.

Now you have insight into how ACO algorithms work and how to use them to solve the carnival problem.

The following section describes some other possible use cases.

Perhaps these examples will help you find uses for the algorithm in your work.

Use cases for ACO algorithms.

ACO algorithms are versatile and useful in several real-world applications.

These applications usually center on complex optimization problems, such as the following: Route optimization.

Routing problems usually include several destinations that need to be visited with several constraints.

In a logistics example, perhaps the distance between destinations, traffic conditions, types of packages being delivered, and times of day are important constraints that we must consider to optimize the operations of the business.

We can use ACO algorithms to address this problem.

The problem is similar to the carnival problem explored in this chapter, but the heuristic function is likely to be more complex and context-specific.

Job scheduling.

Job scheduling is present in almost any industry.

Nurse shifts are important to ensure that good health care is provided.

Computational jobs on servers must be scheduled in an optimal manner to maximize the use of the hardware without waste.

We can use ACO algorithms to solve these problems.

Instead of looking at the entities that ants visit as locations, we see that ants visit tasks in different sequences.

The heuristic function includes constraints and desired rules specific to the context of the jobs being scheduled.

Nurses, for example, need days off to prevent fatigue, and jobs with high priorities on a server should be favored.

Image processing.

We can use the ACO algorithm for edge detection in image processing.

An image is composed of several adjacent pixels, and the ants move from pixel to pixel, leaving pheromone trails.

Ants drop stronger pheromones based on the pixel color's intensity, resulting in pheromone trails along the edges of objects that contain the highest density of pheromones.

This algorithm essentially traces the outline of the image by performing edge detection.

The images may require pre-processing to decolorize the image to grayscale so that the pixel color values can be compared consistently.

Summary of swarm intelligence: Ants.

See this figure.

7. 群体智能：粒子算法

第七章。

群体智能：粒子。

在本章中，我们将理解粒子群智能算法的灵感来源，理解并解决优化问题，以及设计和实现一个粒子群优化算法。什么是粒子群优化？

粒子群优化是另一种群体算法。

群体智能依赖于许多个体的涌现行为，以集体方式解决困难问题。

我们在第六章看到蚂蚁如何通过使用信息素来找到目的地之间的最短路径。

鸟群是自然界中群体智能的另一个理想例子。

当一只鸟独自飞行时，它可能会尝试多种机动和技巧来保存能量，例如在空中跳跃滑翔，或利用风向来承载它飞往想去的方向。

这种行为表明单个个体具有某种原始水平的智能。

但鸟类也需要在季节之间迁徙。

在冬季，昆虫和其他食物来源减少，合适的筑巢地点也变得稀缺。

鸟类倾向于成群飞往更温暖的地区，以利用更好的天气条件，从而提高生存几率。

迁徙通常不是一次短途旅行，需要移动数千英里才能抵达条件适宜的地区。

当鸟类进行这种长距离飞行时，它们往往会结群。

鸟类结群是因为面对捕食者时，数量多就意味着力量大。

此外，结群也能节省能量。

我们在鸟群中观察到的队形有很多优势。

一只强壮的大鸟领头，当它扇动翅膀时，会为身后的鸟产生升力，这样后面的鸟飞行时就能显著减少能量消耗。

如果方向改变或领头鸟疲惫了，鸟群可以更换领导者。

当某只鸟偏离队形时，它会因空气阻力而飞行更困难，并会自我调整以重回队形。

图七点一展示了一个鸟群队形，你可能见过类似的情景。

图七点一，一个鸟群队形示例。

Craig Reynolds在一九八七年开发了一个模拟程序，用以理解鸟群涌现行为的属性，并使用以下规则来引导群体。

这些规则源自对鸟群的观察。

列队。

个体应转向其邻居的平均航向，以确保群体朝相似方向行进。

凝聚。

个体应朝其邻居的平均位置移动，以维持群体队形。

分离。

个体应避免与邻居过于拥挤，以确保个体不会相撞，从而扰乱群体。

在不同的变体中，还会使用其他规则来尝试模拟群体行为。

图七点二展示了个体在不同场景下的行为，以及为遵循规则而被影响移动的方向。

调整移动是在图中所示三个原则之间取得平衡。

图七点二。

引导群体的规则。

粒子群优化算法涉及一群处于解空间中不同点的个体，它们都使用现实世界的群体概念来寻找空间中的最优解。

本章深入探讨粒子群优化算法的工作机制，并展示如何用它来解决问题。

想象一群蜜蜂四散寻找花朵，并逐渐汇聚到花朵密度最高的区域。

随着更多的蜜蜂找到花朵，就会吸引来越来越多的蜜蜂。

其核心，粒子群优化所包含的正是这个例子，如图七点三。

图七点三，一群蜜蜂正汇聚向目标。

本书前面几章提到过优化问题，比如在迷宫中寻找最优路径，为背包确定最优物品组合，以及在嘉年华中寻找游乐设施间的最优路径。

我们解决了这些问题，但没有深入其背后的细节。

然而，从本章开始，更深入地理解优化问题变得很重要。

下一节将构建你所需的直觉，以便在遇到优化问题时能识别出来。

优化问题：一个略具技术性的视角。

假设我们有几种不同大小的辣椒，通常小辣椒比大辣椒更辣。

如果我们根据大小和辣度把所有辣椒绘制在一张图上，结果可能会像图七点四。

图七点四，辣椒辣度对比辣椒大小。

图七点四描绘了辣椒的大小及其辣度。

如果移除辣椒图片，只绘制数据点并在这些点之间画一条曲线，我们就得到图七点五所示的结果。

如果我们有更多辣椒，就会有更多数据点，曲线也会更精确。

图七点五。

辣椒辣度对比辣椒大小趋势。这个例子可能就是一个优化问题。

如果从左到右寻找最小值，我们会遇到几个比前一个值低的点，但在中间会遇到一个更高的点。

我们该停下吗？

如果停下，就会错过实际的最小值，即最后一个数据点，也就是所谓的全局最小值。

这条近似的趋势线或曲线可以用一个函数来表示，如图七点六所示。

这个函数可以解释为：辣椒的辣度等于函数的结果，其中辣椒的大小由x表示。

图七点六。

一个表示辣椒辣度与辣椒大小关系的示例函数。

现实世界的问题通常有成千上万个数据点，而函数的最小输出值不像本例中这样清晰。

搜索空间巨大且难以靠手算解决。

这个例子只用了辣椒的两个属性来创建数据点，从而得到一条简单的曲线。

如果我们考虑辣椒的另一个属性，比如颜色，数据的表示就会发生显著变化。

现在图表必须以三维形式呈现，趋势也变成一个曲面，而不再是曲线。

一个曲面就像一张在三维空间中弯曲变形的毯子，如图七点七。

这个曲面同样用一个函数表示，但更加复杂。

这个例子变得更有趣了。

我们考察了辣椒的三个属性：大小、颜色和辣度。

因此，我们在三个维度中进行搜索。

如果我们还想包括生长地点呢？

这个属性会使数据的可视化和理解变得更加困难，因为我们在四个维度中搜索。

如果我们再加上辣椒的年龄和种植所用的肥料量，我们就面临一个巨大的六维搜索空间，我们甚至无法想象这个搜索空间会是什么样子。

这种搜索同样由一个函数表示，但这个函数太复杂太难，人脑无法解出。

粒子群优化算法尤其擅长解决困难的优化问题。

粒子分布在多维搜索空间中，协同工作以找到好的最大值或最小值。

粒子群优化算法在以下场景中特别有用：大型搜索空间：存在大量数据点和组合可能性的情况。

高维度搜索空间：在高维度中存在的复杂性，即需要问题的很多个维度才能找到好的解决方案。

练习：以下场景的搜索空间将有多少个维度？

在这个场景中，我们需要根据年平均最低气温来确定一个宜居的好城市，因为我们不喜欢寒冷。

同样重要的是，人口要少于七十万人，因为拥挤的地区可能不方便。

平均房价应尽可能低，而且城市拥有的火车越多越好。

解答：此场景中的问题包含四个维度：平均气温、人口规模、平均房价、火车数量。

粒子群优化算法能解决的问题。

假设我们正在开发一架无人机，并使用多种材料来制造其机身和螺旋桨翼，即使其飞行的桨叶。

通过大量的研究试验，我们发现两种特定材料的不同用量，在提升无人机升力和抵抗强风方面会产生不同的最优性能结果。

这两种材料是用于底盘和用于桨叶的塑料。

任何一种材料过多或过少都会导致无人机性能不佳，但有几种组合能造出性能良好的无人机，而只有一种组合能造出性能格外出色的无人机。

图七点十展示了由塑料制成的部件和由铝制成的部件。

箭头标示了影响无人机性能的力。

简而言之，我们想为某款无人机找到一个良好的塑料与铝的比例，以减少升空时的阻力和在风中的摇摆。

因此，塑料和铝是输入，输出则是无人机最终的稳定性。

我们把理想的稳定性描述为减少起飞时的阻力和风中的摇摆。

图七点十。

无人机问题示例。

铝和塑料比例的精确度很重要，且可能的范围很大。

在这个场景中，研究人员已经找到了铝和塑料比例的函数。

我们将在测试阻力和摇摆的模拟虚拟环境中使用这个函数，以在制造另一个无人机原型之前，为每种材料找到最佳值。

我们也知道这些材料比例的最大值和最小值分别是十和负十。

这个适应度函数类似于一个启发式方法。

在现实中，铝和塑料的值为负会很奇怪，但本例中使用它们是为了演示用来优化这些值的适应度函数。

图七点十一展示了关于铝（x）和塑料（y）比例的适应度函数。

给定x和y的输入值，结果是一个基于阻力和摇摆的性能得分。

图七点十一。

优化铝（x）和塑料（y）的示例函数。

如何才能找到制造一架好无人机所需的铝和塑料用量呢？

一种可能的方法是尝试铝和塑料的每一种数值组合，直到为我们的无人机找到最佳材料比例。

退一步想，想象一下找到这个比例所需的计算量。

如果我们把这些材料视为连续值，即用量可以是十点零一、十点零零一或十点零零零一，那么搜索空间实际上是无限的。

如果我们想高效解决这个问题，尝试暴力破解每一个微小的小数增量在计算上是不可能的。

我们需要一种更聪明的方法来探索这片广阔的天地，所以我们需要计算表七点一中各项的结果。

表七点一。

铝和塑料成分的可能值。

这种计算会针对约束条件之间的每一个可能的数字进行，计算代价高昂，因此现实中不可能暴力破解此问题。

我们需要一种更好的方法。

粒子群优化算法提供了一种方法来搜索大型搜索空间，而无需检查每个维度的每个值。

在无人机问题中，铝是问题的一个维度，塑料是第二个维度，它们共同构成了一个二维搜索空间。

无人机的最终性能不是我们要搜索的维度，而是与该二维空间中每个点相关的适应度得分，或称高度。

把这个问题想象成一个在山上的徒步旅行者。

徒步者向北或向东移动（两个运动维度），试图找到最高峰，即问题的最大适应度。

接下来，我们要确定表示一个粒子所需的数据结构，包括这些结构将包含的与问题相关的具体数据。

状态表示：粒子长什么样？

因为粒子在搜索空间中移动，我们必须定义粒子的概念。

以下属性代表了粒子的概念，如图七点十二。

位置：粒子在所有维度中的位置。

最佳位置：使用适应度函数找到的最佳位置。

速度：粒子当前移动的速度。

图七点十二。

粒子的属性。

表达粒子的Python代码示例。

为满足粒子的这三个属性，在粒子的构造函数中需具备以下属性，以用于粒子群优化算法的各种操作。

暂时不用担心惯性、认知分量和社会分量，它们将在后续章节中解释。

见此代码。

粒子群优化算法生命周期。

设计粒子群优化算法的方法基于所处理的问题空间。

每个问题都有独特的背景和不同的数据表示领域。

此外，对不同问题解决方案的衡量方式也不同。

让我们深入探讨如何设计一个粒子群优化算法来解决无人机问题。

粒子群优化算法的一般生命周期如下：图七点十三。

第一步。

设置粒子。

确定要使用的粒子数量，并将每个粒子初始化到搜索空间中的一个随机位置。

第二步。

计算每个粒子的适应度。

给定每个粒子的位置，确定该粒子在该位置的适应度。

第三步。

更新每个粒子的位置。

利用群体智能的原则，反复更新所有粒子的位置。

粒子将探索搜索空间，然后汇聚到好的解上。

第四步。

再次计算每个粒子的适应度。

再次评估粒子的适应度，以确定它们的新位置是否比之前更好。

第五步。

决定停止准则。

决定粒子何时停止更新，算法何时停止。

第六步。

返回最佳解。

当满足停止条件时，输出群体找到的最佳位置。

图七点十三。

粒子群优化算法的生命周期。

粒子群优化算法相当简单，但第三步的细节尤其复杂。

让我们单独查看每一步，并揭示使算法运作的细节。

设置粒子。

算法从创建特定数量的粒子开始，这个数量在算法的整个生命周期中保持不变。

图七点十四展示了此步骤。

图七点十四。

设置粒子。

初始化粒子时，三个重要因素是：粒子的数量。

粒子数量影响计算量：存在的粒子越多，需要的计算就越多。

此外，拥有更多粒子可能意味着汇聚到全局最佳解需要更长时间，因为更多粒子会被各自的局部最佳解吸引。

另外，问题的约束条件也会影响粒子数量。

一个更大的搜索空间可能需要更多粒子来探索，可以多至一千个，也可以少至四个。

通常，五十到一百个粒子能产生好的解，且计算代价不会太高。

图七点十五。

每个粒子的起始位置。

每个粒子的起始位置应该是所有相应维度中的一个随机位置。

粒子均匀分布在搜索空间中很重要。

如果大部分粒子都在搜索空间的某个特定区域，它们将很难找到该区域之外的解。

每个粒子的起始速度。

虽然速度可以始于零，但标准做法是将速度初始化为小的随机值。

这种随机的启动确保了粒子在第一次迭代中就立刻朝不同方向探索，而不是等待群体找到领导者。

想象一下放飞罐子里的萤火虫。

它们不会掉到地上等着，而是立刻向随机方向四散飞开，以覆盖最大范围。

图七点十五。

四个粒子在一个三维平面中初始位置的可视化图。

表七点二描述了在算法初始化步骤中，每个粒子所封装的数据。

速度为零。

当前适应度和最佳适应度值都为零，因为它们尚未被计算。

表七点二。

每个粒子的数据属性。

群体的规模决定了在搜索覆盖率（更多粒子）和计算速度（更少粒子）之间的权衡。

确保随机数生成是均匀的，数字在约束范围内分布在搜索空间中。

此实现取决于所用随机数生成器的特性。

更新粒子的位置。

算法的更新步骤最为复杂，因为这是奇迹发生的地方。

更新步骤将自然界中群体智能的属性封装进一个数学模型，该模型允许在探索搜索空间的同时，不断趋向好的解。

图七点十九。

更新粒子的位置。

群体中的粒子根据其认知能力和周围环境因素（如惯性和群体行为）来更新自己的位置。

这些因素影响每个粒子的速度和位置。

第一步是理解速度是如何更新的，速度决定了粒子运动的方向和快慢。

群体中的粒子移动到搜索空间中的不同点以寻找更好的解。

每个粒子依赖于自己对好解的记忆以及群体最佳解的知识。

图七点二十展示了粒子在位置更新时在群体中的移动。

图七点二十。

粒子在五次迭代中的移动。

更新粒子速度。

使用三个分量来计算每个粒子的新速度：惯性、认知和社会分量。

每个分量都影响粒子的移动。

我们将先单独查看这些分量，然后再深入了解它们如何组合起来更新速度，并最终更新粒子的位置。

惯性。

惯性分量代表特定粒子对移动或方向改变的阻力，它影响着粒子的速度。

惯性分量由两个值组成：惯性大小和粒子当前的速度。

惯性值是一个介于零和一之间的数字。

见此公式。

高惯性（接近一点零）对应探索：粒子保持其先前的动量。

就像一辆沉重的、飞驰的货运卡车，它具有如此大的动量以至于不容易转弯，这使它能够飞速穿越搜索空间并发现新区域。

低惯性（接近零）对应利用：粒子迅速慢下来。

就像一只家蝇，几乎没有动量，可以迅速改变方向，使其能够在迄今找到的最佳解周围徘徊并微调其搜索。

认知。

认知常数是一个大于零且小于二的数。

较大的认知常数鼓励个体独立性或个人探索，防止粒子盲目跟随群体，并确保它彻底检查自己发现点周围的区域。

见此公式。

社会。

社会分量代表粒子与群体互动的能力。

粒子知道群体中的最佳位置，并利用此信息影响其移动。

社会加速由一个常数确定，并通过一个随机数进行缩放。

社会常数在算法生命周期内保持不变，而随机因子则鼓励在偏重社会因素时保持多样性。

见此图。

社会常数越大，利用或收敛就越多，因为粒子会更看重群体的最佳发现，而非自己的。

你可以把这些常数想象成在定义群体的性格。

高认知，低社会。

粒子们变成怀旧的独行侠：它们四处漫游，却不断被拉回自己过往的个人胜利，无视群体。

群体四散开来。

低认知，高社会。

粒子们变成潮流追随者：它们盲目地冲向当前的领导者。

群体迅速坍缩到一个点上，这个点可能是个陷阱或局部最优。

更新速度。

现在我们理解了惯性、认知和社会这三个分量，让我们看看如何将它们结合起来，为粒子更新一个新的速度。

图七点二十一。

假设一个粒子是位正在决定下一步去哪里的游客，它的方向由三个争论的声音决定：惯性，是习惯："我们继续朝已经在走的方向前进吧，转弯太费劲了。"认知记忆："嘿，还记得昨天我们发现的那个超棒的地方吗？

我们回那里去吧。"社会，是同伴压力："瞧，其他人都在朝那座山走呢，那边肯定有什么有趣的事。"图七点二十一。

计算速度的公式。

最终速度就是这三种冲动的简单相加。

光看数学，我们可能觉得很难理解函数中不同分量是如何影响粒子速度的。

图七点二十二描绘了不同因素如何影响一个粒子，表七点四显示了计算每个粒子的适应度后，每个粒子的属性。

图七点二十二。

各因素影响速度更新的直观解释。

表七点四。

每个粒子的数据属性。

接下来，我们将根据刚推导过的公式，深入分析一个粒子的速度更新计算。

以下是为此场景设定的常量配置：惯性设为零点二，此项设置有利于较慢的探索。

认知常数设为零点三五，因为此常数小于社会常数，社会分量比单个粒子的认知分量更受偏爱。

社会常数设为零点四五，因为此常数大于认知常数，社会分量更受偏爱。

粒子们更看重群体找到的最佳值。

图七点二十三描述了粒子四对速度更新公式中惯性、认知和社会分量的计算。

现在，让我们看看，有了每个粒子最佳位置和群体新最佳位置的新信息后，速度更新计算会是什么样子。

粒子们在多次迭代中移动到不同的位置。

图七点二十六描绘了粒子的移动及其向一个解的收敛。

粒子在搜索空间中移动的可视化图。

在图七点二十六的最后一帧中，所有粒子都已收敛到搜索空间中的一个特定区域。

来自群体的最佳解将被用作最终解。

在现实世界的优化问题中，我们不可能将整个搜索空间可视化，这会使优化算法变得不再必要。

为了演示，我们使用了一个标准的数学基准测试函数，即booth函数。

为什么要用测试函数？

在现实世界的问题中，我们很少预先知道完美答案。

然而，为了测试像粒子群优化（PSO）这样的算法是否有效，科学家们使用特定的数学形状，比如booth函数，其全局最小值是已知的，在本例中，它在x等于一、y等于三的位置。

这使我们能够验证我们的群体是否收敛到了正确的目标，如图七点二十七。

图七点二十七。

粒子收敛与已知曲面的可视化图。

在为无人机问题使用了粒子群优化算法后，我们发现，能最小化阻力和摇摆的铝与塑料的最优比例是一比三，即一份铝和三份塑料。

当我们将这些值代入适应度函数，结果是零，这也是该函数的最小值。

更新粒子速度的Python代码示例。

更新步骤可能看起来令人生畏，但如果将这些分量分解成简单、专注的函数，代码就会变得更简单，更容易编写、使用和理解。

首先是惯性计算函数、认知加速函数和社会加速函数。

决定停止准则。

群体中的粒子不能无限地更新和搜索下去，需要确定一个停止准则，让算法运行合理的迭代次数，以找到一个合适的解。

图七点二十八。

图七点二十八。

算法是否已达到停止条件？

迭代次数会影响寻找解的几个方面，包括以下内容：探索。

粒子需要时间来探索搜索空间，以找到存在更优解的区域。

探索也受到速度更新函数中定义的常数的影响。

利用。

在进行合理的探索之后，粒子应收敛到一个好的解上。

停止算法的一种策略是检查群体中的最佳解，并判断它是否已经停滞。

当最佳解的值不再变化，或变化不显著时，就发生了停滞。

变化不显著。

在这种情况下，运行更多迭代并不能帮我们找到更好的解。

当最佳解停滞时，可以调整更新函数中的参数，以偏向更多的探索。

如果想要更多的探索，这种调整通常意味着更多的迭代次数。

停滞可能意味着找到了一个好的解，也可能意味着群体被困在了局部最佳解上。

如果在开始时发生了足够的探索，且群体是逐渐停滞的，那么群体就已收敛到一个好的解上。

图七点二十九。

图七点二十九。

探索、收敛与利用。

粒子群优化算法的用例。

粒子群优化算法很有趣，因为它们模拟了一种自然现象，这也使它们更容易理解，但它们可以应用于不同抽象层次的一系列问题。

本章探讨了无人机制造中的一个优化问题，但粒子群优化算法可以与其他算法结合使用，在寻找好的解方面发挥虽小但关键的作用。

粒子群优化算法一个有趣的应用是深度脑刺激。

这个概念涉及将带有电极的探针植入人脑，以刺激大脑来治疗诸如帕金森病等病症。

每个探针都包含多个电极，可以配置不同的方向，以针对每位患者正确治疗。

明尼苏达大学的研究人员开发了一种粒子群优化算法，来优化每个电极的方向，以最大化感兴趣区域，最小化避让区域，并最小化能量消耗。

由于粒子在搜索这些多维问题空间方面很有效，粒子群优化算法对于为探针上的电极找到最优配置很有效。

图七点三十。

图七点三十。

深度脑刺激中探针所涉及因素的示例。

以下是粒子群优化算法在现实世界中的其他一些应用：优化人工神经网络（ANN）中的权重。

人工神经网络是模仿人脑工作方式的一种构想而建模的。

神经元将信号传递给其他神经元，每个神经元在传递信号前都会对其进行调整。

人工神经网络使用权重来调整每个信号。

该网络的强大之处在于找到正确的权重平衡，来对数据关系中的模式进行建模。

调整权重在计算上代价高昂，因为搜索空间巨大。

想象一下，必须对十个权重的每一种可能的小数组合都进行暴力破解，这个过程将花费数年。

如果这个概念听起来令人困惑，不必惊慌，我们将在第九章探讨人工神经网络是如何运作的。

我们可以使用粒子群优化算法更快地调整神经网络的权重，因为它能在搜索空间中寻找最优值，而无需穷举尝试每一个值。

视频中的运动追踪。

在计算机视觉中，对人体进行运动追踪是一项具有挑战性的任务。

其目标是仅利用视频中图像的信息，来识别人的姿势，并推断出一种运动。

即使人们的关节活动方式相似，但每个人的运动方式各不相同。

由于图像包含许多方面，为预测一个人的运动，搜索空间变得很大，维度也很多。

粒子群优化算法在高维度搜索空间中表现良好，并能提升运动追踪与预测的性能。

音频中的语音增强。

录音是细致入微的，背景噪声总是存在，并可能干扰录音中说话人的内容。

一个解决方案是，从录制的语音音频片段中移除噪声。

为此目的一种技术是，用噪声过滤音频片段，并比较相似的声音，以从音频片段中移除噪声。

这个方案仍然是复杂的，因为降低某些频率可能对音频片段的部分内容有益，却可能劣化其他部分。

为了良好的噪声移除，必须进行精细的搜索和匹配。

传统方法速度慢，因为搜索空间很大。

粒子群优化算法在大搜索空间中表现良好，并能加快从音频片段中移除噪声的过程。

群体智能总结：粒子。见此图。

英文原文

Chapter 7.

Swarm Intelligence, Particles.

In this chapter, Understanding the inspiration for Particle Swarm Intelligence Algorithms Understanding and Solving Optimization Problems Designing and Implementing a Particle Swarm Optimization Algorithm What is Particle Swarm Optimization?

Particle swarm optimization is another swarm algorithm.

Swarm intelligence relies on emergent behavior of many individuals to solve difficult problems as a collective.

We saw in chapter 6 how ants can find the shortest paths between destinations through their use of pheromones.

Bird flocks are another ideal example of swarm intelligence in nature.

When a single bird is flying, it might attempt several maneuvers and techniques to preserve energy, such as jumping and gliding through the air, or using wind currents to carry it in the direction it wants to travel.

This behavior indicates some primitive level of intelligence in a single individual.

But birds also need to migrate between seasons.

In winter, insects and other types of food are less available, and suitable nesting locations become scarce.

Birds tend to flock to warmer areas to take advantage of better weather conditions, improving their likelihood of survival.

Migration usually isn't a short trip.

It takes thousands of miles of movement to arrive at an area with suitable conditions.

When birds travel these long distances, they tend to flock.

Birds flock because there is strength in numbers when facing predators.

Also, flocking saves energy.

The formation we observe in bird flocks has many advantages.

A large, strong bird takes the lead, and when it flaps its wings, it creates uplift for the birds behind it.

Then those birds can fly while using significantly less energy.

Flocks can change leaders if the direction changes or if the leader becomes fatigued.

When a specific bird moves out of formation, it experiences more difficulty in flying via air resistance and corrects its movement to get back into formation.

Figure 7.1 illustrates a bird flock formation.

You may have seen something similar.

Figure 7.1, an example bird flock formation.

Craig Reynolds developed a simulator program in 1987 to understand the attributes of emergent behavior in bird flocks and used the following rules to guide the group.

These rules are extracted from observations of bird flocks.

Alignment.

An individual should steer in the average heading of its neighbors to ensure that the group travels in a similar direction.

Cohesion.

An individual should move toward the average position of its neighbors to maintain the formation of the group.

Separation.

An individual should avoid crowding with its neighbors to ensure that individuals don't collide, which would disrupt the group.

Additional rules are used in different variants attempting to simulate swarm behavior.

Figure 7.2 illustrates the behavior of an individual.

Individual in different scenarios, as well as the direction in which it is influenced to move to obey a rule.

Adjusting movement is a balance of the three principles shown in the figure.

Figure 7.2.

Rules that guide a swarm.

PSOs involve a group of individuals at different points in the solution space, all using real-life swarm concepts to find an optimal solution in the space.

This chapter dives into the workings of the PSO algorithm and shows how to use it to solve problems.

Imagine a swarm of bees that spreads out looking for flowers and gradually converges on the area that has the greatest density of flowers.

As more bees find the flowers, more of them are attracted to the flowers.

At its core, this example is what PSO entails, Figure 7.3.

Figure 7.3, a bee swarm converging on its goal.

Several earlier chapters of this book have mentioned optimization problems, such as finding the optimal path through a maze, determining the optimal items for a knapsack, and finding the optimal path between attractions in a carnival.

We worked through them without diving into the details behind them.

From this chapter on, however, a deeper understanding of optimization problems is important.

The next section builds the intuition you need to identify optimization problems when they arise.

Optimization problems, a slightly more technical perspective.

Suppose that we have several peppers of different sizes.

Usually small peppers are spicier than large ones.

If we plot all the peppers on a chart based on size and spiciness, the result may look like Figure 7.4.

If we had more peppers, we'd have more data points, and the curve would be more accurate.

Pepper Size Trend This example could potentially be an optimization problem.

If we search for a minimum from left to right, we come across several points less than the previous ones, but in the middle, we encounter a higher point.

Should we stop?

If so, we miss the actual minimum, the last data point, known as the global minimum.

The approximated trend line or curve can be represented by a function like the one shown in Figure 7.6.

This function can be interpreted as the spiciness of the pepper equaling the result of the function, where the size of the pepper is represented by x.

Real-world problems typically have thousands of data points, and the minimum output of the function is not as clear as in this example.

The search spaces are massive and difficult to solve.

By hand.

This example uses only two properties of the pepper to create the data points, resulting in a simple curve.

If we consider another property of the pepper, such as color, the representation of the data changes significantly.

Now the chart has to be represented in 3D, and the trend becomes a surface instead of a curve.

A surface is like a warped blanket in three dimensions, figure 7.7.

pepper color.

This example gets more interesting.

We've looked at three attributes of a pepper: size, color, and spiciness.

As a result, we're searching in three dimensions.

What if we want to include the location of growth?

This attribute would make it even more difficult to visualize and understand the data because we're searching in four dimensions.

If we add the pepper's age and the amount of fertilizer used to grow it, we're left with a massive search space in six dimensions, and we can't imagine what this search might look like.

This search is also represented by a function, but one that's too complex and difficult for a person to solve.

PSO algorithms are particularly good at solving difficult optimization problems.

Particles are distributed over the multi-dimensional search space and work together to find good maximums or minimums.

PSO algorithms are particularly useful in the following scenarios.

Large search spaces.

There are many data points and possibilities of combinations.

Search spaces with high dimensions.

There is complexity in high dimensions.

Many dimensions of a problem are required to find a good solution.

Exercise: how many dimensions will the search space for the following scenario have?

In this scenario, we need to determine a good city to live in based on the average minimum temperature during the year, because we don't like the cold.

It's also important that the population be less than 700,000 people, because crowded areas can be inconvenient.

The average property price should be as low as possible, and the more trains the city has, the better.

Solution: The problem in this scenario consists of four dimensions: average temperature, size of population, average price of property, number of trains.

Problems that PSO algorithms can solve.

Suppose that we're developing a drone, using several materials.

To create its body and propeller wings, the blades that make it fly.

Through many research trials, we found that different amounts of two specific materials yield different results in terms of optimal performance for lifting the drone and resisting strong winds.

These two materials are aluminum, for the chassis, and plastic, for the blades.

Too much or too little of either material will result in a poor performing drone.

But several combinations yield a good performing drone, and only one combination results in an exceptionally well-performing drone.

Figure 7.10 illustrates the components made of plastic and the components made of aluminum.

The arrows illustrate the forces that influence the performance of the drone.

In simple terms, we want to find a good ratio of plastic to aluminum for a version of the drone that reduces drag during lift and decreases wobble in the wind.

Therefore, plastic and aluminum are the inputs, and the output is the resulting stability of the drone.

Let's describe ideal stability as reducing drag during liftoff and wobble in the wind.

Figure 7.10.

The example drone problem,Precision in the ratio of aluminum and plastic is important, and the range of possibilities is large.

In this scenario, researchers have found the function for the ratio of aluminum and plastic.

We'll use this function in a simulated virtual environment that tests drag and wobble to find the best values for each material before we manufacture another prototype drone.

We also know that the maximum and minimum ratios for the materials are 10 and minus 10, respectively.

This fitness function is similar to a heuristic.

Negative numbers for aluminum and plastic would be bizarre in reality, but we're using them in this example to demonstrate the fitness function used to optimize these values.

Figure 7.11 shows the fitness function for the ratio between aluminum, x, and plastic, y.

The result is a performance score based on drag and wobble, given the input values for x and y.

Figure 7.11.

The example function for optimizing aluminum, x and plastic y.

How can we find the amounts of aluminum and plastic required to create a good drone?

One possibility is to try every combination of values for aluminum and plastic until we find the best ratio of materials for our drone.

Take a step back and imagine the amount of computation required to find this ratio.

If we treat the materials as continuous values, meaning that the amount could be 10.0, 10.01, or 10.0001, the search space becomes effectively infinite.

Trying to brute force every tiny decimal increment is computationally impossible if we want to solve this problem efficiently.

We need a smarter way to navigate this vast landscape, so we need to compute the result for the items in Table 7.1.

Table 7.1.

Possible values for aluminum and plastic compositions.

This computation goes on for every possible number between the constraints and is computationally expensive, so it's realistically impossible to brute force this problem.

We need a better approach.

PSO provides a means to search a large search space without checking every value in each dimension.

In the drone problem, aluminum is one dimension of the problem, and plastic is the second dimension.

Together, these form a 2D search space.

The resulting performance of the drone isn't a dimension we search through, but the fitness score, or height, associated with each point in that 2D space.

Think of this problem as being like a hiker on a mountain.

The hiker moves north or east, two dimensions of movement, to try to find the highest peak, maximum fitness for the problem.

Next, we determine the data structures required to represent a particle, including the problem-specific data that these structures will contain.

Representing state: what do particles look like?

Because particles move across the search space, we must define the concept of a particle.

The following properties represent the concept of a particle, figure 7.12.

Position, the position of the particle in all dimensions.

Best position, the best position found using the fitness function.

Velocity, the current velocity of the particle's movement.

Figure 7.12.

Properties of a particle.

PSO algorithm lifecycle.

The approach to designing a PSO algorithm is based on the problem space being addressed.

Each problem has a unique context and a different domain in which data is represented.

Also, solutions to different problems are measured differently.

Let's dive into how to design a PSO to solve the drone problem.

The general life cycle of a PSO algorithm is as follows: Figure 7.13.

1.

Set up the particles.

Determine the number of particles to be used, and initialize each particle to a random position in the search space.

2.

Calculate the fitness of each particle.

Given the position of each particle, determine the fitness of that particle at that position.

3.

Update the position of each particle.

Repetitively update the position of all the particles using principles of swarm intelligence.

Particles will explore the search space and then converge on good solutions.

4.

Calculate the fitness of each particle again.

Evaluate the fitness of the particles again to determine whether their new positions are better.

Than their previous ones.

5.

Determine the stopping criteria.

Determine when the particles stop updating and the algorithm stops.

6.

Return the best solution.

Output the best position found by the swarm when the stopping condition is met.

Figure 7.13.

The lifecycle of a PSO algorithm.

The PSO algorithm is fairly simple, but the details of step 3 are particularly intricate.

Let's look at each step in isolation and uncover the details that make the algorithm work.

Set up particles.

The algorithm starts by creating a specific number of particles, which will remain the same for the lifetime of the algorithm.

Figure 7.14 illustrates this step.

Figure 7.14.

Set up the particles.

The three factors that are important in initializing the particles are number of particles.

The number of particles influences computation.

The more particles exist, the more computation is required.

Also, having more particles likely means that converging on a global best solution will take longer because more particles are attracted to their local best solutions.

In addition, the constraints of the problem affect the number of particles.

A larger search space may need more particles to explore it, as many as 1,000 or as few as 4.

Usually, 50 to 100 particles produce good solutions without being too computationally expensive.

Figure 7.15.

Starting position for each particle.

The starting position for each particle should be a random position in all the respective dimensions.

It's important for the particles to be distributed evenly across the search space.

If most of the particles are in a specific region of the search space, they'll struggle to find solutions outside that area.

Starting velocity for each particle.

Although it's possible to start at zero, standard practice is to initialize velocity to small random values.

This random kick ensures that particles explore different directions immediately in the first iteration, rather than wait for the swarm to find a leader.

Think of releasing fireflies from a jar.

They don't drop to the ground and wait, they immediately scatter in random directions to cover the most ground.

Figure 7.15.

A visualization of the initial positions of four particles in a 3D plane.

Table 7.2 describes the data encapsulated by each particle in the initialization step of the algorithm.

The velocity is zero.

The current fitness and best fitness values are zero because they haven't been calculated yet.

Table 7.2.

Data attributes for each particle.

Python code sample for generating a swarm.

The method of generating a swarm consists of creating an empty list and appending new particles to it.

The key factors are ensuring that the number of particles is configurable.

The size of the swarm determines the trace.

Between search coverage, more particles, and computational speed, fewer particles.

Ensuring that the random number generation is done uniformly.

Numbers are distributed across the search space within the constraints.

This implementation depends on the features of the random number generator used.

Ensuring that the constraints of the search space are specified.

In this case, minus 10 and 10 are specified for both x and y of the particle.

Update positions of particles.

The update step of the algorithm is the most intricate because it's where the magic happens.

The update step encompasses the properties of swarm intelligence in nature into a mathematical model that allows exploration of the search space while honing in on good solutions.

Figure 7.19 Figure 7.19.

Update the positions of the particles.

Particles in the swarm update their position given a cognitive ability and factors in the environment around them, such as inertia and what the swarm is doing.

These factors influence the velocity and position of each particle.

The first step is understanding how velocity is updated.

The velocity determines the direction and speed of movement of the particle.

The particles in the swarm move to different points in the search space to find better solutions.

Each particle relies on its memory of a good solution and the knowledge of the swarm's best solution.

Figure 7.20 illustrates the movement of the particles in the swarm as their positions are updated.

Figure 7.20.

The movement of particles over five iterations.

Updating particle velocity.

Three components are used to calculate the new velocity of each particle: inertia, cognitive, and social.

Each component influences the movement of the particle.

We'll look at the components in isolation before diving into how they're combined to update the velocity, and ultimately, position of a particle.

Inertia.

The inertia component represents the resistance to movement or change in direction for a specific particle that influences its velocity.

The inertia component consists of two values: the inertia magnitude and the current velocity of the particle.

The inertia value is a number between zero and one.

See this figure.

High inertia, closer to 1.0, translates to exploration.

The particle maintains its previous momentum.

Like a heavy speeding freight truck, it has so much momentum that it can't turn easily, allowing it to fly through the search space and discover new regions.

Low inertia, closer to zero, translates to exploitation.

The particle slows quickly.

Like a housefly, it has almost no momentum and can change direction quickly, allowing it to hover and fine-tune its search around the best solutions found so far.

Cognitive.

The cognitive constant is a number greater than zero and less than two.

A greater cognitive constant encourages individual independence, or personal exploration, preventing the particle from following the swarm blindly and ensuring that it thoroughly checks the area around its own discoveries.

See this figure.

Social.

The social component represents the ability of a particle to interact with the swarm.

A particle knows the best position in the swarm and uses this information to influence its movement.

Social acceleration is determined by using a constant and scaling it with a random number.

The social constant remains the same for the lifetime of the algorithm, and the random factor encourages diversity in favoring the social factor.

See this figure.

The greater the social constant, the more exploitation or convergence there will be because the particle favors the swarm's best findings over its own.

You can think of these constants as defining the personality of the swarm.

High cognitive, low social.

The particles become nostalgic loners.

They roam around but keep getting pulled back to their own personal victories, ignoring the group.

The swarm spreads out.

Low cognitive, high social.

The particles become trend followers.

They rush blindly toward the current leader.

The swarm collapses quickly into a single point, which may be a trap or local optimum.

Updating velocity.

Now that we understand the inertia, cognitive, and social components, let's look at how they can be combined to update a new velocity for the particles.

Figure 7.21.

Suppose that a particle is a tourist deciding where to go next.

Its direction is determined by three arguing voices.

Inertia, habit.

Let's keep moving in the direction we're already going.

It takes effort to turn.

Cognitive memory.

Hey, remember that great spot we found yesterday?

Let's go back there.

Social peer pressure.

Look, everyone else is heading toward that hill.

Something interesting must be happening there.

Figure 7.21.

Formula to calculate velocity.

The final velocity is simply the sum of these three urges.

By looking at the math, we may find it difficult to understand how the different components in the function affect the velocity of the particles.

Figure 7.22 depicts how the different factors influence a particle, and table 7.4 shows the attributes of each particle after the fitness of each is calculated.

Figure 7.22.

The intuition of the factors influencing velocity updates.

Table 7.4.

Data attributes for each particle.

Here are the constant configurations set for this scenario.

Inertia is set to 0.2.

This setting favors slower exploration.

Cognitive constant is set to 0.35.

Because this constant is less than the social constant, the social component is favored over an individual particle's cognitive component.

Social constant is set to 0.45.

Because this constant is more than the cognitive constant, the social component is favored.

Particles put more weight on the best values found by the swarm.

In real-world optimization problems, it's impossible to visualize the entire search space, which would make optimization algorithms unnecessary.

To demonstrate, we used a standard mathematical benchmark known as the booth function.

Why use a test function?

In real-world problems, we rarely know the perfect answer ahead of time.

To test whether an algorithm like PSO works, however, scientists use specific mathematical shapes, like the booth function, where the global minimum is already known, in this case at x equals 1, y equals 3.

This allows us to verify that our swarm converged on the correct target, figure 7.27.

Figure 7.27.

Visualization of convergence of particles and a known surface.

After using the PSO algorithm for the drone problem, we find that the optimal ratio of aluminum and plastic to minimize drag and wobble is 1 to 3, that is, 1 part aluminum and 3 parts plastic.

When we feed these values into the fitness function, the result is 0, which is the minimum value for the function.

Determine the stopping criteria.

The particles in the swarm can't keep updating and searching indefinitely.

A stopping criterion needs to be determined to allow the algorithm to run for a reasonable number of iterations to find a suitable solution.

Figure 7.28.

Figure 7.28.

Has the algorithm reached a stopping condition?

The number of iterations influences several aspects of finding solutions, including the following.

Exploration.

Particles require time to explore the search space to find areas with better solutions.

Exploration is also influenced by the constants defined in the velocity update function.

Exploitation.

Particles should converge on a good solution after reasonable exploration occurs.

A strategy to stop the algorithm is to examine the best solution in the swarm and determine whether it's stagnating.

Stagnation occurs when the value of the best solution doesn't change or doesn't change by a significant amount.

Amount.

Running more iterations in this scenario won't help us find better solutions.

When the best solution stagnates, the parameters in the update function can be adjusted to favor more exploration.

If more exploration is desired, this adjustment usually means more iterations.

Stagnation could mean that a good solution was found or that the swarm is stuck on a local best solution.

If enough exploration occurred at the start and the swarm gradually stagnates, the swarm has converged on a good solution.

Figure 7.29.

Figure 7.29.

Exploration converging and exploiting.

Use cases for PSO algorithms.

PSO algorithms are interesting because they simulate a natural phenomenon, which makes them easier to understand, but they can be applied to a range of problems at different levels of abstraction.

This chapter looked at an optimization problem for drone manufacturing, but PSO algorithms can be used in conjunction with other algorithms, playing a small but critical role in finding good solutions.

One interesting application of a PSO algorithm is deep brain stimulation.

The concept involves installing probes with electrodes into the human brain to stimulate it to treat conditions such as Parkinson's disease.

Each probe contains electrodes that can be configured in different directions to treat the condition correctly per patient.

Researchers at the University of Minnesota have developed a PSO algorithm to optimize the direction of each electrode to maximize the region of interest, minimize the region of avoidance, and minimize energy use.

Because particles are effective in searching these multi-dimensional problem spaces, the PSO algorithm is effective for finding optimal configurations for electrodes on the probes.

Figure 7.30.

Figure 7.30.

Example of factors involved for probes in deep brain stimulation.

Here are some other real-world applications of PSO algorithms.

Optimizing weights in an artificial neural network, ANN.

ANNs are modeled on an idea of how the human brain works.

Neurons pass signals to other neurons, and each neuron adjusts the signal before passing it on.

An ANN uses weights to adjust each signal.

The power of the network is finding the right balance of weights to model patterns in relationships of the data.

Adjusting weights is computationally expensive because the search space is massive.

Imagine having to brute force every possible decimal number combination for 10 weights.

That process would take years.

Don't panic if this concept sounds confusing.

We explore how ANNs operate in chapter 9.

We can use PSO to adjust the weights of neural networks faster because it seeks optimal values in the search space without exhaustively attempting each one.

Motion tracking in videos.

Motion tracking of people is a challenging task in computer vision.

The goal is to identify the poses.

Of people and imply a motion by using the information from the images in the video alone.

People move differently, even though their joints move similarly.

Because the images contain many aspects, the search space becomes large, with many dimensions to predict the motion for a person.

PSO works well in high-dimension search spaces and can improve the performance of motion tracking and prediction.

Speech enhancement in audio.

Audio recordings are nuanced.

Background noise is always present and may interfere with what someone is saying in the recording.

A solution is to remove the noise from recorded speech audio clips.

A technique used for this purpose is filtering the audio clip with noise and comparing similar sounds to remove the noise from the audio clip.

This solution is still complex because reducing certain frequencies may be good for parts of the audio clip, but may deteriorate other parts of it.

Fine searching and matching must be done for good noise removal.

Traditional methods are slow because the search space is large.

PSO works well in large search spaces and can speed the process of removing noise from audio clips.

Summary of Swarm Intelligence, Particles See this figure.

8. 机器学习

第八章。

机器学习。

本章内容涵盖：用机器学习算法解决问题，掌握机器学习生命周期，准备数据并选择算法。

理解并实现用于预测的线性回归算法。

理解并实现用于分类的决策树学习算法。

什么是机器学习？

机器学习可能看起来是个令人生畏的概念，但只要有正确的理解框架，并理解了过程和算法，它就会变得有趣且引人入胜。

假设你正在寻找一套新公寓。

你询问了朋友和家人，并在网上搜索了市内的公寓。

你注意到不同区域的公寓价格不同。

以下是你从所有研究中得出的一些观察结果：市中心的一套一居室公寓，靠近工作地点，每月租金五千美元。

市中心的一套两居室公寓，每月租金七千美元。

市中心的一套带车库的一居室公寓，每月租金六千美元。

市郊的一套一居室公寓，需要通勤上班，每月租金三千美元。

市郊的一套两居室公寓，每月租金四千五百美元。

市郊的一套带车库的一居室公寓，每月租金三千八百美元。

你注意到了一些模式：市中心的公寓最贵，通常月租在五千到七千美元之间。

市郊的公寓更便宜。

增加房间数，月租会涨一千五到两千美元，而带车库，月租会涨八百到一千美元。

图八点一。

图八点一。

不同区域的房产价格与特征示意图。

这个例子展示了如何利用数据寻找模式并做出决策。

如果你遇到一套市中心带车库的两居室公寓，有理由推测其月租大约为八千美元。

机器学习的目标，就是在数据中寻找模式，以应用于现实世界。

我们能在这个小数据集中发现模式，而机器学习能在庞大复杂的数据集中为我们发现模式。

图八点二描绘了数据不同属性之间的关系。

每个点代表一套独立的房产。

我们看到，越靠近市中心，点越密集，而且与月租价格存在清晰的模式。

价格随着距市中心距离的增加而逐渐下降。

月租价格与房间数之间也存在一种模式。

底部点簇与顶部点簇之间的间隔表明，价格会显著跳升。

我们可能天真地假设，这种影响可能与距市中心的远近有关。

机器学习算法能帮助我们证实或推翻这个假设。

我们将在本章深入探讨这个过程是如何运作的。

图八点二。

数据关系的可视化示例。

通常，数据用表格表示。

列被称为数据的特征，而行被称为样例。

当我们比较两个特征时，被测量的特征有时用Y表示，而变化的特征则归为X。

通过处理一些问题，我们将更好地理解这些术语的直觉含义。

机器学习能解决的问题。

只有当你拥有数据，并能提出数据可能回答的问题时，机器学习才有用。

机器学习算法能在数据中寻找模式，但不能魔法般地做有用的事。

不同类别的机器学习算法采用不同方法来回答问题。

这些大类是：监督学习、无监督学习和强化学习。

图八点三。

图八点三。

机器学习的分类及用途。

监督学习。

传统机器学习中最常用的技术之一就是监督学习。

我们想查看数据，理解数据间的模式和关系，并在给定相同格式的新样例数据时，预测其结果。

找公寓问题就是一个使用监督学习寻找模式的例子。

当我们输入搜索内容并自动补全时，或当音乐应用根据我们的活动和偏好推荐新歌时，我们也会在现实中看到这个例子。

监督学习有两个子类别：回归涉及在数据点集合中画一条线，以最贴合数据的整体形状。

回归可用于诸如寻找与营销活动和销售相关的趋势等应用。

例如：在线广告营销与产品实际销售之间存在直接关系吗？

它也可以用来确定影响某事物的因素。

例如：时间与加密货币价值之间存在直接关系吗？加密货币的价值会随时间指数级增长吗？

分类旨在根据样例的特征来预测其类别。

例如：我们能否根据车轮数量、重量和最高速度，来判定某物是汽车还是卡车？

无监督学习。

无监督学习旨在寻找数据中那些可能通过手动检查难以发现的底层模式。

无监督学习对于将具有相似特征的数据聚类，以及发现数据中重要的特征非常有用。

在电子商务网站上，产品可能根据顾客的购买行为进行聚类。

例如，如果许多顾客同时购买肥皂、海绵和毛巾，那么很可能更多顾客会想要这个产品组合，因此肥皂、海绵和毛巾会被聚类，并推荐给新顾客。

强化学习。

强化学习受行为心理学启发，它通过根据算法在环境中的行为对其进行奖励或惩罚来运作。

它与监督学习和无监督学习有相似之处，也有许多不同。

强化学习旨在基于奖励和惩罚，在环境中训练一个智能体。

想象一下，用零食奖励宠物的良好行为，它因特定行为受到的奖励越多，就越会表现出那种行为。

我们将在第十章讨论强化学习。

遵循机器学习工作流。机器学习不仅仅是关于算法，它通常关乎数据的背景、数据的准备以及所提出的问题。

我们可以通过两种方式发现问题：我们可以通过收集正确的数据，用机器学习解决问题。

假设一家银行拥有大量关于合法和欺诈交易的数据，它想训练一个模型来回答这个问题：我们能实时检测到欺诈交易吗？

我们可以在特定背景下使用数据解决多个问题。

一家农业公司可能拥有不同地区的天气和土壤成分数据，以及各种植物所需的营养数据。

该公司可能会问：我们能在不同类型的数据中找到哪些关联和关系？

找到这些关系可能会引出一个更具体的问题，比如：我们能根据某个地方的天气和土壤，确定种植特定植物的最佳地点吗？

可以把机器学习生命周期想象成炖菜，它包含以下步骤：第一步。

收集数据。

你去市场买食材，你需要新鲜、优质的食材，也就是数据，才能做出好菜。

第二步。

准备数据。

你清洗蔬菜，削土豆皮，切洋葱，你不能把生的、脏的食材直接扔进锅里，你得先清洗并处理它们。

第三步。

训练模型。

这一步是实际的烹饪过程，你混合食材，加热，品尝炖菜。

第四步。

测试模型。

你把炖菜端给顾客。

他们喜欢吗？

是不是太咸了？

第五步。

提高准确度。

你根据顾客反馈调整食谱，下次，你可能会少放盐，或者炖得更久一些。

图八点四提供了一个典型机器学习工作所涉及步骤的简化视图。

这个工作流是迭代的，你经常会根据结果回到之前的步骤，来改进数据或模型。

图八点四：机器学习实验和项目的工作流。

收集并理解数据。

收集和理解你所处理的数据，对于机器学习项目的成功至关重要。

如果你在金融行业的特定领域工作，了解该领域的术语、流程和数据，对于获取能最佳回答你所要实现目标的问题的数据至关重要。

例如，如果你想构建一个欺诈检测系统，理解存储了哪些交易数据及其含义，对于识别欺诈交易至关重要。

你可能还需要从各种系统获取数据，并有效地组合它们。

有时，你所使用的数据会通过组织外部的数据进行增强，以提高准确性。

在本节中，一个关于钻石尺寸测量的示例数据集，将帮助你理解机器学习工作流并探索各种算法。

图八点五。

图八点五。

钻石测量术语。

表八点一描述了几颗钻石及其属性。

X、Y和Z描述了钻石的长度、宽度和深度，以毫米为单位。

价格以美元标示，本例仅使用部分数据。

表八点一，钻石数据集。

该钻石数据集包含十列数据，称为特征，完整数据集有超过五万行。

各特征含义如下：重量，指钻石的重量，一克拉等于二百毫克。

切工。

钻石的品质，等级为：Fair（一般）、Good（良好）、Very Good（非常好）、Premium（优质）或Ideal（理想）。

颜色。

钻石的颜色，范围从D到J，其中D是最好的颜色，J是最差的。

D表示完全无色的钻石，J表示发蒙的钻石。

净度：按品质递减的钻石瑕疵等级，FL、IF、VVS1、VVS2、VS1、VS2、SI1、SI2、I1、I2和I3。

不必担心理解这些代号，它们仅仅代表不同的完美程度。

深度。

深度的百分比，从钻石的底尖到台面测量。

通常，台宽比对于钻石的闪光美感很重要。

台面：钻石平坦顶面相对于X尺寸的百分比。

价格：钻石出售时的价格。

X，钻石X方向的尺寸，以毫米为单位。

Y，钻石Y方向的尺寸，以毫米为单位。

Z，钻石Z方向的尺寸，以毫米为单位。

请记住这个数据集，我们将用它来了解机器学习算法是如何准备和处理数据的。

准备数据：清洗和整理。

现实世界的数据从来都不适合直接处理，数据可能来自各种系统和组织，它们可能遵循不同的数据完整性标准和规则。

你总会遇到缺失数据、不一致数据，以及格式难以适配你想用算法的数据。

表八点二展示了带有缺失和不一致数据的钻石数据集样本。

重要的是要理解，列是数据的特征，每一行是一个样例。

表八点二。

带有缺失和不一致数据的钻石数据集。

缺失数据。

在表八点二中，样例四的切工和深度特征值缺失，样例十二的克拉、台面和Y值缺失。

为了比较样例，我们必须对数据有完全的了解，而缺失值使我们难以获得完全的了解。

机器学习项目的一个目标可能就是估算这些值。

假设缺失数据会带来问题，以下是处理缺失数据的一些方法。

移除缺失数据。

移除那些特征值缺失的样例，也就是本例中的样例四，如表八点三。

这种方法的好处是数据更可靠，因为没有做任何假设，但被移除的样例可能对我们试图实现的目标很重要。

表八点三。

带有缺失数据的钻石数据集。

移除样例。

用均值或中位数替换缺失数据。

另一个选择是用相应特征的均值或中位数来替换缺失值。

均值的计算方法是：将所有值相加，再除以样例的个数。

中位数的计算方法是：将样例按值升序排列，并选择中间的那个值。

使用均值简单高效，但没有考虑特征之间可能存在的相关性，此外，你不能对类别特征使用这种方法，例如钻石数据集中的切工、净度和深度特征。

表八点四。

表八点四。

带有缺失数据的钻石数据集。

要计算台面特征的均值，应把所有可用值相加，再将总和除以所用值的个数。

见此公式。

对缺失值使用台面均值似乎合理，因为台面尺寸看起来没有剧烈差异，但可能存在我们没看到的关联，比如台面尺寸和钻石宽度（X尺寸）之间的关系。

另一方面，使用克拉均值则毫无道理，因为如果我们将数据绘制到图表上，会发现克拉特征与价格特征之间存在关联。

价格似乎随着克拉值的增加而上涨。

用众数替换缺失数据。

你可以用该特征最常出现的值（也就是数据的众数）来替换缺失值。

这种方法适用于类别特征，但没有考虑特征间可能存在的关联，并且可能因使用最高频出现的值而引入偏差。

进阶方法。

采用统计学方法，使用K近邻（KNN）或神经网络。

K近邻利用数据的多个特征来寻找一个估算值。

与K近邻类似，只要有足够的数据，神经网络也能准确地预测缺失值，然而，这两种算法在处理缺失数据时计算代价都很高。

进阶方法。

不做任何处理。

有些算法，比如XGBoost，无需准备就能处理缺失数据，但我们将在本章探讨的算法则不行。

模糊值。

另一个问题是，有些值含义相同但表示方法不同。

钻石数据集中的例子是第二、九、十和十二行，其切工和净度特征的值是小写而非大写，我们能知道这一点，仅仅是因为我们理解这些特征及其可能的值。

如果没有这些知识，我们可能会把Fair和fair看作不同的类别。

要解决这个问题，我们可以将值标准化为大写或小写，以保持一致性。

表八点五。

表八点五。

带有模糊数据的钻石数据集。

标准化值。

编码类别数据。

因为计算机和统计模型处理的是数值，我们在建模时，会对处理字符串值和类别值（比如fair、good、SI1、I1）感到困难。

我们需要将这些类别值表示为数值。

以下是完成此任务的方法。

独热编码。

可以想象成使用一排开关，除了一盏亮着，其余全部关闭，亮着的那盏代表该位置上特征的存在。

如果我们用独热编码表示切工，切工特征就变成了五个特征，除了代表每个样例切工值的那一列为一，其余值都是零。

为节省空间，表八点六中已移除其他特征。

表八点六。

带有独热编码值的钻石数据集。

标签编码。

标签编码将每个类别表示为零到类别总数之间的一个数字。

仅将此方法用于评级或与评级相关的标签，否则，你训练的模型会假定该数字对样例具有权重意义，并可能引入非预期的偏差。

练习。

识别并修复本例中的问题数据。

决定可以用哪些数据准备技术来修复以下数据集，决定删除哪些行，对哪些值使用均值，以及如何编码类别值。

本练习中的数据集与你目前处理过的数据集略有不同。

见此表。

解答。

修复此数据集的一种方法包括以下三个任务。

删除第八行，因为其产地缺失。

我们不知道这个数据集将用于什么目的，如果产地特征很重要，而此行在清洗后被删除，可能会导致问题。

另一种选择是，如果该特征与其他特征有关系，我们可以估算其值。

用独热编码对产地值进行编码。

之前，我们使用标签编码将字符串值转换为数值。

这个方法当时有效，因为这些值代表的是更优的切工、净度或颜色等级。

而在产地这个例子中，其值标识的是钻石的开采地。

如果使用标签编码，我们就会给数据集引入偏差，因为在这个数据集中，没有任何一个产源地点优于其他地点。

为缺失值求均值。

第一、二、四和五行分别缺失Y、X、台面和Z的值。

使用均值应该是一种好技巧，因为尺寸和台面特征是相关的。

测试与训练数据。

在开始训练线性回归模型之前，我们必须确保有数据用来教导（训练）模型，也有一些数据用来测试它在预测新样例方面表现如何。

回想一下找公寓的问题。

在了解了影响价格的各个属性之后，我们可以通过观察距离和房间数来做出价格预测。

在本例中，我们将使用一个新表作为训练数据，因为我们有更多真实数据可供稍后测试。

训练模型。

用线性回归进行预测。

选择使用哪种算法主要取决于两个因素：所提出的问题以及可用数据的性质。

如果问题是预测一个特定克拉重量钻石的价格，那么回归算法会很有用。

算法的选择还取决于数据集中的特征数量以及这些特征之间的关系。

如果数据有很多维度，需要考虑很多特征才能做出预测，我们可以考虑多种算法和方法。

回归意味着预测一个连续值，例如钻石的价格或克拉数。

"连续"意味着这些值可以是某个范围内的任何数字，例如，价格"二千二百七十一美元"就是一个在零和任何钻石最高价之间的连续值，回归可以帮助预测它。

线性回归是最简单的机器学习算法之一，它能找到两个变量之间的关系，并允许我们通过已知一个变量来预测另一个。

一个例子是基于钻石的克拉值来预测其价格。

通过观察许多已知钻石的样例，包括它们的价格和克拉值，我们可以教会一个模型这种关系，并让它做出预测。

給数据拟合一条线。

让我们尝试在数据中寻找趋势，并尝试做出一些预测。

为了探索线性回归，我们提出的问题是：钻石的克拉数和价格之间是否存在关联？如果存在，我们能做出准确预测吗？

我们首先分离出克拉和价格特征，并将这些数据点绘制在图上。

因为我们想基于克拉值寻找价格，所以将克拉视为X，价格视为Y。

克拉是自变量，即X。

自变量是在实验中为确定其对因变量的影响而被改变的量。

在这个例子中，就是调整克拉的值来确定具有该克拉值钻石的价格。

价格为因变量，即Y。

因变量是被测试的那个变量，它受自变量影响，并随自变量的值变化而改变。

在我们的例子中，我们感兴趣的是给定一个特定克拉值时的价格。

图八点六将克拉和价格数据绘制在图表上。

表八点七描述了这些数据。

图八点六。

克拉与价格数据的散点图。

表八点七，克拉与价格数据。

请注意，与价格相比，克拉的数值非常小。

价格高达数千，而克拉则在十进制小数范围内。

为便于理解本章学习中的计算，我们可以缩放克拉值，使其与价格值可比。

通过将每个克拉值乘以一百，我们得到的数字在随后的推演中更容易手动计算。

通过缩放所有行，我们并没有影响数据中的关系，因为每个样例都应用了相同的操作。

由此生成的数据（图八点七）在表八点八中表示。

图八点七，克拉与价格数据的散点图。

表八点八。

调整克拉值后的数据。

求特征的均值。

寻找回归线的第一步是找到每个特征的均值，也就是所有值的总和除以值的个数。

克拉数的均值是一千二百二十九，在X轴上用垂直线表示。

价格均值是三千四百三十一美元，在Y轴上用水平线表示，如图八点八。

图八点八。

用垂直线和水平线表示的X和Y的均值。

均值之所以重要，是因为从数学上讲，我们找到的任何回归线，都将穿过X均值和Y均值的交点。

许多直线都可能穿过这一点。

有些回归线在拟合数据方面可能比其他线更好。

最小二乘法旨在创建一条线，以最小化该线与数据集中所有点之间的距离。

这个方法是寻找回归线的常用方法。

图八点九展示了几条回归线的示例。

图八点九。

几条可能的回归线。

用最小二乘法寻找回归线。

但回归线的目的是什么呢？

假设我们要修建一条尽可能靠近所有主要办公楼的地铁线。

让一条地铁线途经每一栋楼是不可行的，那个方案需要太多的车站，而且耗资巨大。

相反，我们将尝试创建一条尽可能缩短到每栋楼距离的直线路线。

有些人可能不得不比别人多走点路，但这条直线对每栋办公楼的人来说都是最优化的。

这正是我们在做的事情，也是回归线所要实现的目标。

这些建筑物就是数据点，而这条线就是笔直的地铁路线。

图八点十。

图八点十。

回归线的直观理解。

线性回归总能找到一条拟合数据的直线，以从总体上最小化各点之间的距离。

理解直线方程很重要，因为我们将学习如何找到描述一条直线的变量的值。

一条直线由方程y等于c加m乘以x来表示，如图八点十一。

y，因变量。

x，自变量。

M，直线的斜率。

C，直线在y轴上的截距。

图八点十一。

表示一条直线的方程的直观理解。

我们使用最小二乘法来找到回归线。

在高层面上，这个过程包含图八点十二中描绘的步骤。

要找到最接近数据的线，我们需要找出实际数据值与预测数据值之间的差异。

各个数据点的差异会各不相同：有些会很大，有些会很小；有些会是负的，有些会是正的。

通过对差异进行平方并求和，我们考虑了所有数据点的全部差异。

最小化这个平方差的总和，就是最小二乘法要得到一条好的回归线的目标。

想象一下，在你的数据点间画一条线。

现在，对每个点，画一个将点连接到线的完美正方形。

有些正方形会很小，有些则可能很大。

算法的目标就是最小化所有这些正方形的总面积，这就是它被称为"最小二乘"的原因。

这也解释了为什么单个异常值如此危险：一个距离十单位远的点，不仅给误差增加了十，还产生了一个高达一百的巨大的正方形，迫使线条大幅移动以缩小它。

图八点十二说明了这个过程。

如果这个图看起来有点令人却步，不用担心，我们会逐步讲解每一步。

图八点十二。

计算回归线的基本工作流。

至此，我们的线已知一些变量了。

例如，我们知道一个x值是幺二二九，一个y值是三四三一，如第二步所示。

接下来，我们计算每个克拉值与克拉均值之差，以及每个价格值与价格均值之差，以求出x减x均值，以及y减y均值，这将在第三步用到，见表八点九。

表八点九。

钻石数据集与计算，第一部分。

对于第三步，我们还需要计算每个克拉值与克拉均值之差的平方，以求出x减x均值的平方。

我们还需要对这些值求和，以进行最小化，该总和等于三百七十万三千六百九十，见表八点十。

表八点十。

钻石数据集与计算，第二部分。

第三步方程中最后缺失的值，是x减x均值乘以y减y均值的值。

同样，需要求这些值的总和，总和等于一千一百六十二万四千三百七十，见表八点十一。

表八点十一，钻石数据集与计算，第三部分。

现在我们可以将计算出的值代入最小二乘方程来计算m了。

见此方程。

既然我们有了m的值，就可以通过代入x和y的均值来计算c。

请记住，所有回归线都会通过这个点，所以它是回归线上的一个已知点。

见此？

最后，我们可以通过生成一些介于最小值和最大值之间的克拉值，把它们代入代表回归线的方程，然后绘制出这条线，如图八点十三。

见此。

与数据点一同绘制的回归线。

我们基于自己的数据集训练出了一条能够精确拟合数据的线性回归线。

至此，我们亲手完成了一些机器学习工作。

测试模型，确定模型的准确度。

既然我们已经确定了一条回归线，我们就可以用它来对其他克拉值进行价格预测了。

我们可以用我们知道实际价格的新样本来衡量回归线的性能，并确定线性回归模型的准确度如何。

我们不能用训练模型时所用的相同数据来测试它，因为这种做法会导致高准确率，但毫无意义。

训练好的模型必须用模型未曾见过的真实数据来测试。

分离训练和测试数据。

训练数据和测试数据通常按八二比例分开，即百分之八十的可用数据用作训练数据，百分之二十用于测试模型。

使用百分比，是因为很难知道精确训练一个模型所需的样例数量，具体场景和所提问题可能需要或多或少的数据。

图八点十四和表八点十二表示钻石示例的一组测试数据。

请记住，我们曾缩放克拉值，使其与价格值相似。

所有克拉值都被乘以了一千，以便于阅读和处理。

这些点代表测试数据点，而这条线代表训练好的回归线。

图八点十四。

与数据点一同绘制的回归线。

表八点十二。

克拉与价格数据。

测试模型包括用未曾见过的训练数据进行预测，然后将模型的预测准确度与实际值进行比较。

在钻石示例中，我们知道实际的价格值，因此我们将确定模型预测的结果，并比较其差异。

衡量回归线的性能。

在线性回归中，衡量模型准确度的一种常用方法是计算R平方。

R平方用于确定实际值与预测值之间的方差。

我们使用以下方程来计算R平方得分。

见此方程。

正如在训练步骤中一样，我们首先需要计算实际价格值的均值，计算实际价格值与价格均值之间的距离，然后计算这些值的平方。

表八点十三。

我们正使用本章前文图八点十四中绘制为点的那些值。

表八点十三。

钻石数据集与计算。

下一步是为每个克拉值计算预测的价格值，将这些值平方，并计算所有这些值的总和。

表八点十四。

表八点十四。

钻石数据集与计算，第二部分，利用"预测价格与均值之差的平方和"以及"实际价格与均值之差的平方和"，我们可以计算出R平方得分。

见此方程。

结果是，R平方得分为零点八八，这意味着我们的模型解释了目标变量——钻石价格——中百分之八十八的方差。

通俗地讲，钻石价格中百分之八十八的差异可以用我们纳入的特征（如重量和切工）来解释，剩余的百分之十二归因于其他因素或噪声，这表明模型对数据的拟合度很强。

这个结果相当不错，表明线性回归模型是相当准确的。

对于钻石示例来说，这个结果是令人满意的。

判断该准确度对于我们试图解决的问题是否足够，取决于问题所在的领域。

我们将在下一节探讨机器学习模型的性能。

提示。

关于拟合数据线的入门介绍，请参见Paul Orland所著《面向程序员的数学》第十四章，链接在此。

线性回归可以应用于更多维度。

例如，我们可以通过一个称为多元回归的过程，来确定钻石的克拉、价格和切工值之间的关系。

这个过程增加了一些计算的复杂性，但基本原理保持不变。

提高准确度。

在数据上训练了模型，并衡量了它在新的测试数据上的表现后，我们就对模型的表现有了个概念。

通常，模型的表现不如预期，如果可能的话，我们必须做额外的工作来改进模型。

这种改进涉及对机器学习生命周期中的各个步骤进行迭代。

图八点十五。

复习一下机器学习生命周期。

结果可能需要我们关注一个或多个方面。

机器学习是一项实验性工作，我们要在不同阶段测试不同策略，然后才确定表现最佳的方法。

在钻石示例中，如果使用克拉值预测价格的模型表现不佳，我们可以将表示尺寸的钻石维度与克拉值结合使用，以尝试更准确地预测价格。

以下是提高模型准确度的一些方法。

收集更多数据。

一种解决方案可能是，收集更多与我们所探索的数据集相关的数据，也许是用相关的外部数据增强数据，或是纳入我们先前未考虑的数据。

以不同方式准备数据。

我们可能必须以不同方式准备用于训练的数据，因为所选方法可能会产生错误。

我们可能必须使用不同的技术来寻找缺失数据的值、替换模糊数据以及编码类别数据。

选择数据中的不同特征。

数据集中的其他特征可能更适合预测因变量。

例如，X维度值可能是预测台面值的好选择，因为它与台面值有物理上的关系，如本章前文图八点五中的钻石术语图所示，而用X维度预测净度值则毫无意义。

使用不同的算法来训练模型。

有时，所选算法不适合要解决的问题或数据的性质。

我们可以使用不同的算法来实现我们的目标，这将在下一节讨论。

处理假阳性测试。

当我们在检查泛化能力时，评估指标可能会具有欺骗性。

一个模型可能在训练数据上获得高准确度得分，但当面对新的、未见过数据时，它可能表现很差。

这种差异通常是由过拟合造成的，请参见侧边栏。

如果线性回归没有提供有用的结果，或者我们有不同的问题要问，我们可以尝试一系列其他算法。

下一节将探讨针对不同问题可使用的算法。

过拟合与欠拟合。

当模型与训练数据过于紧密对齐，而没有足够的灵活性去处理方差更大的新数据时，就会发生过拟合。

可以把过拟合想象成，通过死记硬背练习题的具体答案来备考。

你可能在练习测试中得了满分，但在真正的考试中却不及格，因为考题的措辞略有不同。

相比之下，欠拟合就是根本没有学习。

你既通不过练习测试，也通不过真正的考试。

如果你做到了良好拟合，你就理解了概念。

你可能会遗漏一些个别的细节，但你能适应并解决你从未见过的问题。

用决策树进行分类。

简单来说，分类问题涉及根据样例的属性为其分配一个标签。

这些问题与回归不同，回归是估算一个值。

让我们深入探讨分类问题，并看看如何解决它们。

分类问题：非此即彼。

我们知道，回归涉及基于一个或多个其他变量来预测一个值，例如给定钻石的克拉值来预测其价格。

分类与之相似，它也旨在预测一个值，但它预测的是离散的类别，而不是连续值。

离散类别是数据集的类别特征，如钻石数据集中的切工、颜色和净度，这与连续值（如价格和深度）相对。

考虑另一个例子。

假设我们有数种车辆：小汽车和卡车。

我们测量每辆车的重量和车轮数量。

我们暂且忽略小汽车和卡车外观可能很不同这一点。

例如，几乎所有小汽车都只有四个轮子，但许多大卡车有超过四个轮子。

此外，卡车通常比小汽车重，但大型运动型多用途车可能和小型卡车一样重。

我们或许可以找到重量和车轮数量之间的关系，来预测一辆车是小汽车还是卡车，如图八点十六。

图八点十六。

基于车轮数量和重量进行潜在分类的车辆示例。

练习：回归对比分类。

考虑以下场景，并判断每个属于回归问题还是分类问题。

基于有关老鼠的数据，我们有一个预期寿命特征和一个肥胖特征，我们正试图找到这两个特征之间的关联。

基于有关动物的数据，我们有每只动物的重量，并且知道它是否有翅膀，我们正试图确定哪些动物是鸟。

基于有关计算设备的数据，我们拥有数种设备的屏幕尺寸、重量和操作系统，我们想要确定哪些设备是平板电脑、笔记本电脑或手机。

基于有关天气的数据，我们有降雨量和湿度值，我们想要确定不同降雨季节的湿度。

让我们来辨别每个场景的问题类型。

回归。

我们在探索两个变量之间的关系，预期寿命是因变量，肥胖是自变量。

分类。

我们正利用样例的重量和翅膀特征，将样例分类为鸟或非鸟。

分类。

我们正利用设备的其他特征，将样例分类为平板电脑、笔记本电脑或手机。

回归。

我们在探索降雨量与湿度之间的关系，湿度是因变量，降雨量是自变量。

决策树基础。

我们对回归和分类问题使用不同的算法。

一些流行的算法包括支持向量机、决策树和随机森林。

在本节中，我们将研究决策树算法来学习分类。

决策树是描述为找到问题解决方案所做的一系列决策的结构。

图八点十七，一个基本决策树的示例。

使用决策树就像玩猜谜游戏"二十一道题"，一个玩家想着某样东西，另一个玩家通过提问来缩小思考范围并正确猜出答案。

算法的目标是通过选择一个能排除最多错误答案的问题，来以尽可能少的问题赢得游戏。

以下是坏问题和好问题的示例：坏问题。

"它是一个物体吗？"这个问题对数据的划分很差，因为几乎所有东西都是物体。

好问题。

"它是有生命的吗？"这个问题完美地将数据划分为有生命和无生命的事物。

例如，如果我们今天在决定是否穿短裤，我们可能会做出一系列的决定来得出结果。

今天会冷吗？

如果不冷，我们晚上会在外面待到很晚，届时会变冷吗？

天暖和我们可以决定穿短裤，但如果要在一个寒冷的夜晚外出，就不会穿了。

对于钻石示例，我们将尝试使用决策树，基于克拉和价格值来预测钻石的切工。

为简化此例，假设我们是钻石经销商，并且不关心每个具体的切工等级。

我们将把这些切工归为两个更宽泛的类别："Fair"和"Good"切工将被归入一个叫"OK"的类别，而"Very Good"、"Premium"和"Ideal"切工将被归入一个叫"Perfect"的类别。

现在，我们的样本数据集看起来像表八点十五。

见此表。

表八点十五。

用于分类示例的数据集。

通过查看这个小示例中的值，并凭直觉寻找模式，我们可能会发现一个规律：价格似乎在零点九八克拉之后显著飙升，而增长的价格似乎与"Perfect"钻石相关，而克拉值较小的钻石倾向于"OK"。

但属于"Perfect"的样例三，其克拉值很小。

图八点十八展示了，如果我们创建问题来筛选数据并手动分类，会发生什么。

决策节点包含我们的问题，叶节点包含我们已分类的样例。

图八点十八。

通过人类直觉设计的决策树示例。

对于这个小数据集，我们可以轻松地手动将钻石分类。

然而，真实世界的数据集包含成千上万个需要处理的样例，可能还有成千上万个特征，这使得一个人几乎不可能靠手动创建决策树。

这就是决策树算法派上用场的地方。

决策树可以创建用于筛选样例的问题。

决策树能发现人类可能忽略的复杂模式，并以人类无法达到的规模处理数据。

但原始能力并不总意味着更好的结果。

决策树太急于寻找模式，以至于它们很容易过拟合，即死记硬背数据，除非我们施加特定的约束来保持它们的泛化性。

训练决策树。

要创建一棵足够智能、能在钻石分类中做出正确决策的树，我们需要一个能从数据中学习的训练算法。

有一个用于决策树学习的算法家族，我们将使用其中一个特定的算法，名为CART，即分类与回归树。

CART以及其他树学习算法的基础是：决定要问什么问题以及何时问这些问题，以最佳方式将样例筛选到它们各自的类别中。

在钻石示例中，算法必须学会就克拉和价格值提出最佳问题，以及何时提问，以最好地区分"OK"和"Perfect"钻石。

决策树的数据结构。

要理解树的决策将如何构建，我们可以回顾一下以下数据结构，它们以适合决策树学习算法的方式组织逻辑和数据。

类别映射，即标签分组。

映射是一种键值对的集合，不能有两个相同的键。

这种结构可用于存储匹配特定标签的样例数量，以及计算熵（也称为不确定性）所需的值。

我们将在本章后面学习熵。

节点树。

如前面的树图（图八点十八）所示，多个节点链接起来组成一棵树。

这个示例可能在前面的章节中遇到过。

树中的节点对于筛选、将样例划分到各个类别至关重要。

决策节点。

决策节点是数据集被分割或筛选的地方。

问题：正在被提问的问题是什么？

见下文的问题点。

真样例：满足该问题的样例。

假样例：不满足该问题的样例。

样例节点或叶节点，是包含属于单一类别的样例列表的节点，此列表中的所有样例都应已被正确分类。

问题：问题可以根据其灵活性以不同方式表示。

我们可以问："克拉值大于零点五且小于一点一三吗？"为保持本例简单，问题由一个可变特征、一个可变值以及大于等于运算符构成。

"克拉值大于等于零点五吗？"或"价格大于等于三千零四十五吗？"特征：正在被询问的特征。

值：比较值必须大于或等于的那个常量值。

决策树学习生命周期。

本节讨论决策树算法如何通过决策来筛选数据，以正确分类一个数据集。

图八点十九展示了训练一个决策树所涉及的步骤，本节的其余部分将描述该图中的流程。

图八点十九。

构建决策树的基本流程。

在构建决策树时，我们测试所有可能的问题，以判断在决策树的特定位置，问哪个问题是最好的。

为了测试一个问题，我们使用熵的概念，熵是对数据集中不确定性或不纯度的度量。

假设你有一个袋子，里面装着五颗"Perfect"钻石和五颗"OK"钻石，如图八点二十。

如果你盲目伸手进袋子里，你完全不能确定会拿到什么。

这种五十对五十的均分，代表着高熵，即混乱度最大。

图八点二十。

不确定性的示例。

现在，想象一堆有十颗"Perfect"钻石和零颗"OK"钻石。

这毫无意外可言，你完全知道你将会拿到什么。

这就是低熵，即零混乱度。

决策树的目标，就是提出能分割数据以减少熵的问题，将我们从杂乱、混杂的状态，带到干净、纯粹的状态。

给定一个包含克拉、价格和切工特征的初始钻石数据集，我们可以使用基尼指数来确定该数据集的不确定性，基尼指数是衡量数据不纯度或混乱度的一种度量。

基尼指数为零，意味着数据集没有不确定性，是纯粹的，比如，它可能包含十颗"Perfect"钻石。

图八点二十一展示了基尼指数是如何计算的。

基尼指数为零点五，所以如果随机选取一个样例，有百分之五十的概率会选到一个标记错误的样例，如前文图八点二十所示。

图八点二十一。

基尼指数计算。下一步是创建一个决策节点来分割数据。

决策节点包含一个问题，我们可以用它来合理地分割数据，并降低不确定性。

请记住，零表示没有不确定性。

我们的目标是将数据集划分为不确定性为零的子集。

基于每个样例的每个特征，会生成许多问题，用以分割数据并确定最佳的分割结果。

因为我们有两个特征和十个样例，所以生成的问题总数将是二十个。

图八点二十二展示了一些被提问的问题。

都是些简单的问题，询问某个特征的值是否大于或等于某个特定值。

图八点二十二。

用决策节点分割数据所提问题的示例。

数据集的不确定性由基尼指数确定，而提问旨在降低不确定性。

熵是另一个衡量混乱度的概念，它使用基尼指数，针对基于所提问题进行的特定数据分割进行计算。

我们必须有办法判断一个问题降低不确定性的效果如何，而我们可以通过衡量信息增益来完成这个任务。

信息增益描述了通过提出某个特定问题所获得的信息量。

如果获得的信息很多，不确定性就更小。

计算信息增益的方法，是用提问前的熵，减去提问后的熵。

为此，请遵循以下步骤：第一步。

通过提问来分割数据集。

第二步。

测量左侧分割的基尼指数。

第三步。

将左侧分割的熵与分割前的数据集进行比较测量。

第四步。

测量右侧分割的基尼指数。

第五步。

将右侧分割的熵与分割前的数据集进行比较测量。

第六步。

将左侧熵和右侧熵相加，计算分割后的总熵。

第七步。

用分割前的总熵减去分割后的总熵，计算出信息增益。

图八点二十三展示了针对问题"价格是否大于等于三千九百一十四"的数据分割和信息增益。

在此例中，所有问题的信息增益都会被计算，而信息增益最高的问题，将被选为在树的那个节点处要提问的最佳问题。

然后，原始数据集会根据包含问题"价格是否大于等于三千九百一十四"的决策节点进行分割。

一个包含此问题的决策节点被添加到决策树中，左侧和右侧的分割都源于该节点。

图八点二十三。

基于一个问题的数据分割与信息增益示意图。

在图八点二十四中，数据集分割后，左侧只包含纯粹的"Perfect"钻石数据集，而右侧包含一个混合了钻石分类的数据集，包括两颗"Perfect"钻石和五颗"OK"钻石。

必须对数据集的右侧再提一个问题，以进一步分割该数据集。

再次地，利用数据集中每个样例的特征，会生成若干问题。

图八点二十四。

第一个决策节点及可能的问题产生后的最终决策树。

练习。

计算一个问题的不确定性和信息增益。

用你目前从图八点二十三中学到的知识作指导，计算问题"克拉值是否大于等于零点七六"的信息增益。

解答。

下图中描绘的解答，突出显示了计算模式的复用，即给定一个问题，如何确定其熵和信息增益。

请随意练习提出更多问题，并将结果与该图中的信息增益值进行比较。

见此图。

在第二层级基于一个问题进行数据分割与信息增益的示意图。

这个分割、生成问题和确定信息增益的过程，会递归地发生，直到数据集被问题完全分类。

图八点二十五展示了完整的决策树，包括所有被提问的问题以及由此产生的分割。

图八点二十五。

训练好的完整决策树。需要特别注意的是，决策树通常是用更大的数据样本训练出来的。

所提问题必须更具普遍性，才能适应种类更广泛的数据，因此需要各种各样的样例来学习。

信息增益使用左侧和右侧的分割以及当前的不确定性，来确定信息增益。
